# TRACE — CLAUDE.md
## Product, Creative Direction, Design System & Engineering Standards

> This document is the single source of truth for designing and developing Trace. Read it fully before making architectural, visual, or functional changes.
>
> **Primary objective:** Build an outstanding, premium, highly polished web application with the visual refinement of a leading creative development studio, while maintaining Trace's genuine learning functionality.
>
> **Design references:**
> - Primary visual reference: The warm orange and white SaaS landing page supplied by the project owner (Pinterest reference).
> - Creative quality reference: https://www.karma.business/
>
> Do not copy either reference literally. Extract their design principles and apply them to Trace's own identity.

---

# 1. PRODUCT UNDERSTANDING

## What is Trace?

Trace is a coding-learning platform for people who build software using AI but want to understand the resulting code well enough to work independently.

The problem:

Someone uses AI to build a FastAPI backend, an endpoint, or a database integration.

The application works, but when the API fails, a database write does not happen, or a bug appears, the builder might not understand the code's behavior.

They can generate more code with AI, but cannot confidently reason about or modify the existing implementation themselves.

Trace exists to close this gap.

### Core proposition

**You built it. Now understand it.**

Trace helps AI-assisted builders learn to:
- Read and understand realistic code.
- Predict how code will behave.
- Identify bugs and misconceptions.
- Make focused code changes.
- Verify actual execution results.
- Apply previously learned concepts independently.

Trace is not a conventional coding quiz platform, AI code generator, or passive tutorial application.

It is a practice environment focused on understanding real program behavior.

## The learning loop

The core workflow is:

**READ → PREDICT → MODIFY → VERIFY → REVISIT**

### READ
The learner reads a small piece of realistic application code.

### PREDICT
The learner predicts the behavior before seeing the result.

### MODIFY
The learner changes an expression or a small section of code.

### VERIFY
Trace checks the actual behavior using execution results, API responses, or database state.

### REVISIT
Trace presents a related but changed exercise later, helping assess whether the learner retained the underlying concept.

Sessions should typically take approximately 5–10 minutes.

The interface must make these steps immediately understandable.

## Evidence over invented scores

Trace tracks:
- Initial predictions.
- Learner attempts.
- Hint usage.
- Code revisions.
- Verification outcomes.
- Follow-up exercise results.

Do not invent IQ scores, mastery percentages, productivity metrics, or unverified learning claims.

Use evidence such as:

"Correct on first attempt."

"Hint used before revision."

"API returned HTTP 422."

"Database record not created."

"Revisit completed without hints."

The difference between code evidence and learning evidence is fundamental to the product.

---

# 2. DESIGN PHILOSOPHY

## Creative vision

Trace should look like a modern premium technology product designed by an experienced art director and creative frontend engineer.

The visual target combines:

1. Premium editorial typography.
2. Carefully controlled whitespace.
3. Warm atmospheric gradients.
4. Refined nearly white surfaces.
5. High-quality technical product demonstrations.
6. Purposeful motion.
7. Elegant component composition.
8. Clear information architecture.
9. Precise alignment.
10. Strong visual storytelling.

Every section must appear intentionally designed.

The product should feel sophisticated, intelligent, approachable, and technically credible.

## Design concept

**FOLLOW THE SIGNAL**

Trace follows a signal through code:

Input → Execution → Outcome → Understanding.

Represent this idea with:
- Fine connecting lines.
- Subtle active-line highlights.
- Trace paths.
- Numbered learning stages.
- Precise technical labels.
- Carefully animated sequence indicators.
- Clear relationships between code and verified output.

Avoid turning the website into an overly futuristic or cyberpunk dashboard.

The design should remain calm and readable.

## What the reference image teaches us

The supplied Pinterest reference demonstrates a particularly effective composition.

### Outer ambient frame

A warm gradient surrounds the main website.

The gradient includes muted:
- Terracotta.
- Coral.
- Peach.
- Amber.
- Warm cream.

A large off-white inner canvas sits above this environment.

The framing gives the page depth without overwhelming the content.

### Navigation

The navbar is compact, floating, clean, and visually lightweight.

Its interior background is white.

The typography is small but sharp.

The primary CTA uses warm orange.

### Hero composition

The headline sits in the visual center.

The main text is deep graphite.

One important phrase is highlighted orange.

The copy has carefully measured line lengths, spacing, and line breaks.

### Product demonstration

A large application preview appears below the hero.

Soft ambient lighting and subtle vertical light treatments create visual depth behind it.

For Trace, this preview must depict a realistic interactive learning experience, not a banking dashboard.

### Supporting sections

Sections are composed with strong hierarchy and consistent spacing.

Feature cards contain original, purpose-made visuals.

The design uses repetition where useful, but not identical cards everywhere.

**Do not reproduce the original brand, copy, icons, logos, or product screenshots.**

---

# 3. VISUAL DESIGN SYSTEM

## Color palette

Use CSS variables for all colors.

```css
:root {
  --ambient-base: #C88D81;
  --background: #FAFAFC;
  --surface: #FFFFFF;
  --surface-muted: #F4F5F7;

  --foreground: #151923;
  --text-secondary: #717784;
  --text-tertiary: #9196A1;

  --border: #E5E7EC;

  --primary: #FF8139;
  --primary-hover: #E96E29;
  --primary-soft: #FFF0E6;

  --editor-background: #1A1E27;
  --editor-foreground: #E8EAF0;

  --success: #227953;
  --error: #BD4B4B;
  --warning: #A46D2B;
}
```

### Color rules

- Most of the interface must remain nearly white.
- Graphite should be used for primary text.
- Orange is a signature accent, not the color of every element.
- Use orange for important actions and selective highlighted text.
- Ambient gradients should be atmospheric rather than dominant.
- Semantic success and error colors must remain distinguishable.
- Do not use orange to indicate successful verification.
- Maintain WCAG-compliant text contrast.

Avoid:
- Generic purple-blue AI gradients.
- Neon colors.
- Excessive glassmorphism.
- Randomly colored cards.
- Glowing borders around everything.
- Saturated orange sections without a compositional reason.

## Typography

Preferred fonts:

**Display:** Geist Sans or Plus Jakarta Sans.

**Body:** Geist Sans or Inter.

**Code:** Geist Mono or JetBrains Mono.

Use `next/font` wherever supported.

### Hero typography

Desktop size:

```css
font-size: clamp(3.3rem, 7vw, 7rem);
line-height: 0.98;
letter-spacing: -0.055em;
font-weight: 600;
```

Adjust based on actual rendering.

A compelling hero does not require oversized text for its own sake. The headline should feel balanced within the viewport.

### Section headings

```css
font-size: clamp(2.3rem, 4.5vw, 4.75rem);
line-height: 1.08;
letter-spacing: -0.045em;
font-weight: 600;
```

### Body text

- Base size: 16–18px.
- Line-height: 1.5–1.7.
- Comfortable reading width.
- Strong distinction between titles, paragraphs, labels, and supporting details.

### Code

- 13–15px on desktop.
- Approximately 1.6 line-height.
- Maintain indentation and whitespace.
- Use proper syntax highlighting.
- Avoid tiny code text to fit more content.

## Spacing

Use a consistent spacing scale:

4, 8, 12, 16, 20, 24, 32, 40, 48, 64, 80, 96, 120, 160.

Avoid arbitrary margins and padding across unrelated components.

## Containers

```css
:root {
  --page-max: 1440px;
  --content-max: 1200px;
  --reading-max: 720px;
  --gutter: clamp(20px, 4vw, 64px);
  --section-space: clamp(80px, 10vw, 160px);
}
```

Use a consistent container system.

Desktop:
- 12-column layout grid.
- Generous whitespace.
- Controlled text widths.
- Strong alignment between sections.

Tablet:
- Six-column or appropriately simplified grid.

Mobile:
- Single-column composition where required.
- Meaningful changes to structure rather than simply shrinking desktop elements.

## Borders and radii

Use:
- Subtle 1px borders.
- 10–16px standard card radii.
- 20–24px hero product preview radius.
- Carefully selected button radii.
- Soft, minimal shadows.

Avoid using the same giant border radius for every component.

---

# 4. PAGE ARCHITECTURE

Trace should have two related but distinct experiences.

## Marketing website

Purpose:
- Explain the problem.
- Establish differentiation.
- Demonstrate the product.
- Encourage visitors to try a lesson.

Characteristics:
- Editorial.
- Spacious.
- Visually immersive.
- Strong typography.
- Sophisticated movement.
- Carefully art-directed.

## Learning application

Purpose:
- Help users complete genuine exercises.
- Preserve readable code.
- Capture predictions.
- Submit changes.
- Verify results.
- Review progress.

Characteristics:
- Focused.
- Precise.
- Accessible.
- Efficient.
- Lower visual density where possible, but enough density for real technical work.

Both experiences must share typography, colors, UI primitives, and product identity.

Do not force the marketing website's atmospheric gradients into the exercise workspace.

---

# 5. MARKETING HOMEPAGE

Build the homepage as a cohesive visual narrative.

## Section 01 — Floating navigation

Include:
- Trace brand.
- Product.
- How it works.
- Learning approach.
- Sign in where supported.
- Primary CTA.

Recommended CTA:

**Try Trace**

Use a compact rounded white navbar with a subtle border and restrained shadow.

The navigation must remain accessible on mobile.

Use real routes or functional in-page links.

Do not create decorative links with no destination.

## Section 02 — Hero

Recommended eyebrow:

THE GAP BETWEEN SHIPPING AND UNDERSTANDING

Recommended headline:

**You can build with AI.  
Now understand the code.**

Highlight "understand" in orange.

Supporting text:

Trace turns real code into short predict, debug, and verify exercises—so you can work through the next bug yourself.

Primary CTA:
**Try a lesson**

Secondary CTA:
**See how Trace works**

Design requirements:
- Large centered headline.
- Carefully controlled line breaks.
- Compact eyebrow.
- Elegant button grouping.
- Soft atmospheric treatment.
- Generous vertical whitespace.
- High-contrast text.
- Immediate clarity about the product.

Do not add unnecessary decorative illustrations around the headline.

## Section 03 — Hero product showcase

This is one of the most important parts of the entire website.

Show an elegant, realistic Trace application interface below the headline.

The preview should include:

**Code excerpt**

A realistic Python or FastAPI example.

**Prediction question**

Example:

"What happens when the request omits the title field?"

**Response selection**

A learner makes a prediction.

**Code change**

The learner fixes a missing expression or modifies a small section of code.

**Verification evidence**

Display actual outcomes from the application's verification system when available.

Examples:
- HTTP status.
- Test results.
- Response body.
- Database state.

The preview should clearly communicate the learning process.

Visual direction:
- Large rounded application window.
- Refined editor typography.
- Light UI framing.
- Optional dark code panel.
- Delicate line numbers.
- Clear active-state indicators.
- Small technical labels.
- Beautiful but functional controls.
- Subtle shadow.
- Soft ambient glow behind the preview.
- Restrained vertical light accents.

If the preview is only illustrative, label it as a demonstration.

Never show a fabricated result as though it came from a live backend.

## Section 04 — Core principles strip

Communicate four principles:

REAL CODE

PREDICT FIRST

VERIFY WITH EVIDENCE

PRACTISE AGAIN

Use understated typographic treatment with thin separators.

Do not invent partner brands, customer testimonials, or adoption statistics.

## Section 05 — Three feature cards

Create three original, visually sophisticated feature cards.

### Predict what happens

Explain why prediction strengthens understanding.

Visual direction:
- Code excerpt.
- Highlighted execution path.
- Two possible outcomes.
- Delicate directional connectors.

### Make the change

Show a focused code modification.

Visual direction:
- Before and after code fragments.
- Refined diff highlighting.
- A small editable area.
- Meaningful interaction hints.

### See the evidence

Show actual program results.

Visual direction:
- HTTP response.
- Test output.
- Database record state.
- Explicit difference between expected and observed results.

The cards must not all contain the same icon inside a gradient square.

Each needs its own purposeful visual composition.

## Section 06 — Learning loop

Create an engaging visual explanation of:

READ → PREDICT → MODIFY → VERIFY → REVISIT.

Use a connected visual system inspired by tracing the execution of a program.

Each step must clearly explain what the learner does.

Do not overload the section with animation.

## Section 07 — Actual exercise walkthrough

Show a realistic exercise based on a FastAPI backend.

Explain:
1. What the user is attempting.
2. Which code will execute.
3. What the learner predicts.
4. Which code change is required.
5. What the verification checks reveal.
6. Why the actual behavior occurred.

Use realistic code.

Make the walkthrough understandable even when motion is disabled.

## Section 08 — Learning history

Show concrete evidence of improvement.

Example:
- Original prediction.
- Attempted revision.
- Hint usage.
- Verification outcome.
- Follow-up exercise.
- Independent attempt.

Do not use arbitrary mastery percentages.

A learner's history should describe actual work.

## Section 09 — Final CTA

Suggested headline:

**Stop just shipping code.  
Start understanding it.**

Supporting text should reinforce Trace's practical learning value.

Include a working primary CTA.

Finish with a refined minimal footer.

# 6. APPLICATION USER EXPERIENCE

## Lesson workspace

Prioritize:
- Readability.
- Clarity.
- Fast interaction.
- Clear task progression.
- Immediate access to evidence.

Recommended layout on large screens:

Left:
- Lesson context.
- Code excerpt or editor.

Right:
- Prediction.
- Modification task.
- Feedback.
- Verification state.

Use responsive layouts to prevent cramped code.

## Code editor

Use CodeMirror 6 if an editable code environment is required.

Use syntax highlighting for read-only snippets without loading a large editor engine unnecessarily.

Include:
- Consistent monospace typography.
- Accurate indentation.
- Accessible focus behavior.
- Useful line numbers.
- Relevant code highlighting.
- Clear errors.
- Proper selection behavior.

Avoid making the editor look like a decorative terminal.

## Prediction interface

The learner should commit to a prediction before the outcome is shown.

Do not reveal the answer through premature success styling or hints.

Support clear selections and keyboard interaction.

## Modification interface

Allow focused code changes.

The interface must show:
- What can be modified.
- What is expected.
- How to submit.
- What happens when verification runs.

Do not use fake buttons or pretend that code is executed locally when the actual verification occurs in the backend.

## Verification interface

Separate:

**Expected behavior**

What the learner predicted.

**Observed behavior**

What actually happened.

**Explanation**

Why it happened.

Evidence may include:
- HTTP status.
- JSON response.
- Test output.
- Database state.

Verification must originate from the appropriate deterministic system or clearly identified sample data.

## Review and progress

Present:
- Attempts.
- Revisits.
- Hint usage.
- Corrected misconceptions.
- Independent follow-up outcomes.

Avoid unsupported claims about cognitive ability or mastery.

---

# 7. ANIMATION AND MOTION SYSTEM

Motion should elevate the product rather than interrupt it.

## Recommended stack

Use:

- CSS transitions for simple interactions.
- Motion for React for component transitions.
- GSAP with ScrollTrigger only when advanced choreography genuinely requires it.

Do not use multiple animation libraries for the same task.

## Motion principles

1. Motion must have a purpose.
2. Every animated element must remain understandable.
3. Use consistent easing.
4. Avoid excessive delays.
5. Prioritize opacity and transform animations.
6. Respect reduced-motion preferences.
7. Never make learning depend on an animation.
8. Avoid scroll hijacking.

## Timing guidance

Microinteraction:
120–220ms.

UI state transition:
180–350ms.

Section entrance:
400–700ms.

Large visual reveal:
500–900ms when justified.

These are starting guidelines, not fixed values.

## Hero animation

Suggested sequence:
1. Navbar gently appears.
2. Eyebrow fades in.
3. Headline appears with subtle upward movement.
4. Supporting copy appears.
5. CTAs enter.
6. Product preview reveals.

Keep the entire sequence responsive and avoid making users wait to interact.

Do not hide essential content if JavaScript fails.

## Scroll animation

Use small, intentional reveal transitions.

Suitable effects:
- Opacity changes.
- Slight vertical translation.
- Delicate line drawing.
- Staggered card reveals.
- Progressive trace-path highlighting.

Avoid:
- Constant parallax.
- Excessive blur animations.
- Scroll-controlled text that becomes unreadable.
- Decorative animations competing with the code editor.
- Long unskippable intro sequences.

## Button interactions

Buttons should have:
- Immediate hover feedback.
- Clear focus indicators.
- Short transitions.
- Proper disabled states.
- Loading indicators when needed.

## Lesson transitions

Make state changes purposeful.

Examples:
- Predicted outcome becomes committed.
- Code change becomes highlighted.
- Verification completes.
- Expected and observed outcomes become visible.
- The next learning step becomes available.

Use subtle motion to reinforce cause and effect.

## Reduced motion

Implement `prefers-reduced-motion`.

Do not depend on a moving effect to communicate information.

---

# 8. NEXT.JS ENGINEERING ARCHITECTURE

Use the existing project architecture where appropriate.

Preferred stack:

- Next.js App Router.
- React.
- TypeScript.
- Tailwind CSS.
- CSS variables for tokens.
- Radix primitives.
- shadcn/ui when useful.
- Motion for React.
- Lucide icons.
- CodeMirror for editable code.
- FastAPI backend.
- SQLite persistence.

Do not introduce packages merely because they are trendy.

## Component philosophy

Components must be reusable, focused, typed, and maintainable.

Separate:
- Global design primitives.
- Marketing components.
- Learning components.
- Data access.
- Verification presentation.
- Animation utilities.

Suggested organization, subject to repository inspection:

```text
src/
  app/
    layout.tsx
    page.tsx
    globals.css
    learn/
    progress/
  components/
    ui/
    layout/
    marketing/
    learning/
    code/
    evidence/
  lib/
    api/
    utils/
    motion/
  hooks/
  styles/
```

This is a conceptual structure.

Do not destroy or unnecessarily reorganize a functioning repository to match it.

## Server and Client Components

Use Server Components by default.

Use Client Components when:
- Local interactive state is required.
- Browser interaction is required.
- Animation needs client-side behavior.
- A code editor is present.
- Interactive lesson controls are required.

Avoid turning the entire application into one client-rendered tree.

## Backend integration

Trace's FastAPI backend is responsible for:
- Lesson interactions.
- Saved attempts.
- Hints and revisions.
- Learning state.
- Verification logic.
- Follow-up practice.
- Persistence.

The frontend should consume the real backend contracts.

Before modifying the integration:
1. Inspect actual endpoints.
2. Inspect request and response models.
3. Inspect authentication or session behavior.
4. Inspect persistence expectations.
5. Check available tests.
6. Preserve known-working behavior.

Do not invent API routes based solely on this document.

Do not replace backend logic with mock frontend state.

## Loading and error states

Every asynchronous interaction needs:
- Loading state.
- Error state.
- Recovery action.
- Appropriate success feedback.
- Stable layout during changes.

Do not show successful verification before the backend reports success.

## Security

Do not put secrets in client components.

Validate and safely render untrusted content.

Avoid injecting unsanitized HTML.

Do not execute learner-submitted code in an unsafe application process or browser context.

Keep code execution and verification in the existing approved backend system.

---

# 9. RESPONSIVE DESIGN

Responsiveness is a design requirement, not a final CSS adjustment.

Review at:

- 320px.
- 360px.
- 390px.
- 768px.
- 1024px.
- 1280px.
- 1440px.
- 1920px.

## Desktop

- Large editorial hero.
- Floating navigation.
- Balanced product preview.
- Generous whitespace.
- Multi-column feature layouts.
- Clear application workspace.

## Tablet

- Reorganize sections intentionally.
- Maintain readable type.
- Prevent code panels from overflowing.
- Avoid overcrowded navigation.

## Mobile

- Reflow hero text naturally.
- Reposition CTAs.
- Simplify atmospheric effects.
- Stack feature compositions.
- Keep code readable with appropriate horizontal scrolling where needed.
- Avoid overflowing images.
- Make navigation genuinely functional.
- Keep important controls reachable.

For the lesson interface, use tabs or a compact segmented view if necessary:

CODE | TASK | RESULT.

Do not shrink code to unreadable sizes.

---

# 10. ACCESSIBILITY

Target WCAG 2.2 AA.

Requirements:
- Semantic HTML.
- Correct heading order.
- Keyboard navigation.
- Visible focus indicators.
- Accessible form labels.
- Adequate color contrast.
- Reduced-motion support.
- Clear validation messages.
- Screen-reader-compatible evidence.
- Accessible dialogs and menus.
- Proper focus restoration.
- Meaningful button labels.
- No color-only status communication.
- Readable code and technical output.

Use appropriate live regions for verification completion.

Do not announce every animation frame.

---

# 11. PERFORMANCE

The goal is a visually sophisticated website that remains fast.

Targets for field Core Web Vitals at the 75th percentile:

- LCP at or below 2.5 seconds.
- INP at or below 200ms.
- CLS at or below 0.1.

Implementation rules:
- Use `next/font`.
- Use `next/image` for suitable image assets.
- Lazy-load expensive below-the-fold elements.
- Avoid unnecessary JavaScript.
- Keep animation transforms GPU-friendly where appropriate.
- Do not animate layout-heavy properties continuously.
- Dynamically load large editor or graphics libraries.
- Avoid excessive blurred layers.
- Prevent layout shifts.
- Optimize SVGs.
- Avoid large background videos unless justified.

The hero should be readable before JavaScript hydration.

Avoid implementing WebGL or Three.js for effects achievable with lightweight CSS or SVG.

---

# 12. ORIGINAL VISUAL ASSETS

Do not fill the website with generic stock artwork.

Preferred visual ingredients:
- Realistic Trace interface previews.
- Original code diagrams.
- Abstract technical line work.
- Execution traces.
- Verification evidence panels.
- Carefully composed SVG graphics.
- Minimal gradients and lighting.
- Structured code diffs.

If custom graphics are needed, create assets consistent with the product's visual language.

Do not use random emoji icons or generic AI illustrations.

The imagery should explain Trace's learning process.

---

# 13. CONTENT AND COPYWRITING

Write clearly and specifically.

Audience:
- AI-assisted builders.
- Early-stage developers.
- People building real applications.
- Learners seeking practical independence.

Preferred writing:
- Short, useful sentences.
- Technically accurate statements.
- Clear descriptions of real benefits.
- Specific examples.

Avoid:
- "Unlock your limitless potential."
- "Revolutionize your learning journey."
- "10× your coding skills instantly."
- "Guaranteed mastery."
- "The world's most advanced coding platform."

These claims are either vague or unsupported.

Better:

"You can ship an API. Can you explain why it failed?"

"Predict the result before running the code."

"See what the database actually saved."

"Practise again with fewer hints."

"Understand the code you already built."

---

# 14. DESIGN QUALITY CONTROL

A website that compiles is not necessarily a finished website.

Before considering a feature or page complete, inspect the rendered result.

Evaluate:

### Typography
- Does the headline have strong visual impact?
- Are line breaks intentional?
- Is letter spacing refined?
- Are paragraph widths controlled?
- Is the hierarchy consistent?

### Composition
- Is the primary focal point clear?
- Are sections appropriately spaced?
- Does each section have a distinct visual purpose?
- Is there a consistent alignment system?
- Is the page balanced?

### Color
- Does orange provide meaningful emphasis?
- Is the background calm?
- Are gradients controlled?
- Are semantic colors correct?

### Components
- Are buttons aligned?
- Are border radii consistent?
- Are shadows subtle?
- Are inputs usable?
- Are states complete?

### Motion
- Does animation support comprehension?
- Is it smooth?
- Is it restrained?
- Does reduced-motion work?

### Product authenticity
- Is code realistic?
- Are examples understandable?
- Are verification outcomes genuine or clearly labeled as illustrative?
- Are CTAs functional?
- Are learning interactions connected to the backend where available?

### Responsiveness
- Does the page work on narrow screens?
- Are navigation and controls accessible?
- Are code panels readable?
- Is there horizontal overflow outside intended code areas?

### Technical quality
- Are tests passing?
- Are there runtime errors?
- Is the build successful?
- Are existing backend flows intact?

---

# 15. COMMON AI-GENERATED DESIGN FAILURES TO AVOID

Do not create a generic SaaS template.

Specifically avoid:

1. Repeating identical icon cards.
2. Random glowing gradient blobs.
3. Excessive glassmorphism.
4. Generic purple hero backgrounds.
5. Excessively rounded everything.
6. Default Tailwind typography.
7. Fake testimonials.
8. Fake user statistics.
9. Generic code screenshots.
10. Placeholder navigation.
11. Buttons without functionality.
12. Meaningless animated particles.
13. Excessive scroll animation.
14. Decorative dashboards without real content.
15. Arbitrary mastery scores.
16. Inconsistent spacing.
17. Tiny low-contrast text.
18. Overcomplicated component abstractions.
19. Unnecessary dependencies.
20. Replacing working backend functionality with frontend simulations.

The visual quality should come from composition, typography, originality, craft, and attention to detail—not from excessive decoration.

---

# 16. IMPLEMENTATION WORKFLOW FOR CLAUDE CODE

When asked to build or redesign a feature, follow this workflow.

## Step 1 — Audit the repository

Inspect:
- Existing framework.
- Next.js version.
- Folder structure.
- Routes.
- Components.
- Styles.
- Fonts.
- Dependencies.
- Backend integration.
- Tests.
- Existing assets.

Do not assume the repository is empty.

## Step 2 — Define the screen's purpose

Determine:
- Who uses it?
- What is the primary action?
- What information is needed?
- What API data is involved?
- What states must be supported?
- What evidence should be displayed?

## Step 3 — Plan visual composition

Before implementation, identify:
- Main visual hierarchy.
- Container width.
- Grid structure.
- Typography scale.
- Section sequence.
- Visual focal points.
- Responsive strategy.
- Necessary interactions.

Do not begin by adding dozens of unrelated cards.

## Step 4 — Establish the system

Build or refine:
- Design tokens.
- Typography.
- Buttons.
- Navigation.
- Containers.
- Panels.
- Inputs.
- Technical labels.
- Code display.
- Motion utilities.

Reuse shared primitives.

## Step 5 — Implement the experience

Create functional UI components.

Preserve the existing backend.

Where possible, build one complete vertical slice from frontend interaction through backend verification to displayed evidence.

## Step 6 — Add motion

Only after the static design works.

Implement:
- Microinteractions.
- Section reveals.
- Product-demo transitions.
- Verification feedback.
- Reduced-motion alternatives.

## Step 7 — Validate

Run available:
- Lint checks.
- TypeScript checks.
- Frontend tests.
- Backend tests.
- Production build.
- Browser interaction checks.
- Responsive screenshot inspections.

If screenshot or browser tooling is available, inspect the actual page.

Fix visible alignment, wrapping, spacing, and interaction problems.

Do not claim a test passed unless it was actually run.

## Step 8 — Report accurately

At completion, state:
- What was implemented.
- Which files changed.
- What interactions work.
- Which tests were executed.
- What remains incomplete.
- Any integration limitations.

Do not provide generic completion claims without verification.

---

# 17. FIRST HOMEPAGE IMPLEMENTATION PRIORITIES

For the initial implementation, prioritize the following in order.

### Priority 1

Establish the Pinterest-inspired visual foundation:
- Ambient outer gradient.
- Inset white website canvas.
- Typography.
- Floating navigation.
- Orange accent system.
- Layout grid.

### Priority 2

Build a polished hero:

Create an exceptional Trace product showcase:

- Verification evidence.
- Carefully art-directed product frame.

### Priority 4

Build supporting sections:

- Feature cards.
- Learning loop.
- Exercise walkthrough.
- Learning history.
- Final CTA.

### Priority 5

Refine interactions:
- Entrance animation.
- Hover feedback.
- Scroll reveals.
- Mobile navigation.
- Responsive layouts.
- Reduced-motion alternatives.

### Priority 6

Perform final quality checks:
- Browser inspection.
- Desktop and mobile review.
- Functional testing.
- Backend regression checks.
- Visual refinement.

Do not prioritize decorative complexity over core product functionality.

---

# 18. REFERENCE DOCUMENTATION

Use official documentation and follow the installed versions where possible.

Next.js:
https://nextjs.org/docs/app

Next.js Server and Client Components:

Web Vitals:

Creative quality reference:
https://www.karma.business/

---

# 19. FINAL PRINCIPLES

Trace must succeed on two dimensions.

## Design quality

The website should have:
- Exceptional typography.
- Premium composition.
- Strong visual storytelling.
- Carefully considered color.
- Refined motion.
- Cohesive components.
- Original product visuals.
- Excellent responsive behavior.

## Product quality

The application should:
- Teach through realistic code.
- Require genuine learner attempts.
- Capture predictions.
- Support code modifications.
- Show verification evidence.
- Preserve progress.
- Support follow-up practice.
- Avoid false learning claims.

A beautiful homepage with a broken learning experience is not successful.

A working learning tool with a generic, poorly composed interface does not meet our design ambition.

## Final decision rule

Whenever a design or engineering decision is ambiguous, ask:

**Does this help the learner understand code, or does it merely make the interface look impressive?**

Choose clarity and correctness first.

Then elevate that experience through outstanding visual design and deliberate interactions.

**The final objective is to make Trace look like a premium modern software product and behave like a rigorous, evidence-based learning tool. Both are required.**

I saved the document to [CLAUDE.md](CLAUDE.md) exactly as given and read it back in full (1,478 lines). No CLAUDE.md existed at the repo root, so nothing was overwritten. I haven't changed any code.

The main audit finding is that **this repo is an untouched Create Next App starter, and there is no Trace FastAPI backend anywhere on this machine**. So there are no lessons, attempts, verification or SQLite yet, and no tests.

## 1. Trace in my own words

**The problem.** People who build with AI end up with a FastAPI endpoint that works until it doesn't. Then they can't explain why a request returned 422 or why no database row was written. Prompting for more code doesn't fix that gap in understanding.

**The loop.** In short sessions of 5–10 minutes, the learner:
- **reads** a small piece of realistic code;
- **predicts** what it will do and commits to that answer before seeing anything;
- **modifies** one expression or a few lines;
- **verifies** against what actually happened: HTTP status, response body, test output, database state;
- **revisits** the concept later in a changed scenario with less help, to see whether it stuck.

**Evidence over invented scores.** Trace records facts such as "Correct on first attempt", "Hint used before revision", "API returned HTTP 422" or "Revisit completed without hints". It never shows mastery percentages or similar invented scores. Code evidence (what the program did) stays separate from learning evidence (what the learner predicted, tried and later reproduced). Any result not produced by a real run is labelled as a demonstration.

## 2. Rules I'll follow most strictly

1. **Never fake results.** Any evidence that didn't come from the real backend is visibly labelled as a demonstration or sample.
2. **No made-up content.** No invented API routes, metrics, testimonials, logos, statistics or mastery scores.
3. **Prediction comes first.** The learner commits before any outcome is shown, and nothing hints at the answer early.
4. **Every link and button works.** Each CTA goes to a real route or a real in-page anchor; anything unsupported (such as "Sign in") is left out.
5. **Server Components by default.** Client code only where there's real interaction, so the hero is readable before JavaScript loads.
6. **Tokens, not one-off colours.** All colours come from §3's CSS variables. Orange is a sparing accent and never means success.
7. **Accessibility and motion.** WCAG 2.2 AA, keyboard support, visible focus, reduced-motion versions of everything, and no scroll hijacking.
8. **Responsive is part of the design.** Checked at 320–1920px, and the page never scrolls sideways except inside code panels.
9. **Minimal dependencies.** Nothing gets added without a specific job, and I'll check the Next 16.4 docs bundled in `node_modules` before using its APIs.
10. **Honest reporting.** I only report checks I actually ran in this environment.

## 3. Audit findings

| Area | What's there |
|---|---|
| Framework | Next **16.4.3**, React **19.3.0**, TypeScript strict, App Router, `src/` layout, `@/*` alias |
| Config | `cacheComponents`, `reactCompiler`, `partialPrefetching` and `experimental.agentFeedback` are on. Tailwind **4.3.3** loads through `@tailwindcss/turbopack`, with no PostCSS config. |
| Routes | Only `/`, which is the starter page. |
| Components | None: no `components/` or `lib/` folders. |
| Styles | [globals.css](src/app/globals.css) is the starter version: white/near-black colours, a dark-mode switch, and `font-family: Arial` on `body`, which overrides Geist. |
| Fonts | Geist and Geist Mono via `next/font/google` in [layout.tsx](src/app/layout.tsx). This matches the doc. |
| Metadata | Still "Create Next App". |
| Dependencies | Only next, react and react-dom. Motion, Lucide, Radix/shadcn, CodeMirror, Shiki, Playwright and Vitest are all missing. |
| Backend | **None.** No Python files, no SQLite, no API client, no env config. I searched the Desktop and your user folder. The FastAPI projects nearby (`apex`, `PayEase`) are unrelated (orders, pricing, payments). |
| Tests | None, frontend or backend. |
| Assets | Only the starter SVGs (`next.svg`, `vercel.svg`, etc.) and the default favicon. |
| Lint script | `eslint` with Next's core-web-vitals and TypeScript configs. |

**Conflicts with the document:**

## 4. Proposed plan for the first homepage (§17 order)

Before writing code, I'll read the bundled Next docs on fonts, CSS, Server/Client Components and Cache Components.

**Priority 1: visual foundation**
- [globals.css](src/app/globals.css): §3 colour tokens, container and spacing variables, a Tailwind `@theme` mapping, type scale, focus styles and a global reduced-motion rule. Dark mode and Arial are removed.
- [layout.tsx](src/app/layout.tsx): keep Geist and Geist Mono, add real metadata and a skip-to-content link.

- `src/components/layout/site-nav.tsx`: a floating white navbar with in-page anchors (Product, How it works, Learning approach) and a "Try Trace" CTA.

**Priority 2: hero.** `src/components/marketing/hero.tsx` is a Server Component using the doc's eyebrow, headline (with "understand" in orange), supporting line and two CTAs.

**Priority 3: product showcase.** This is the signature piece.
- `src/content/demo-lesson.ts`: one source for the sample FastAPI `POST /notes` code, the prediction options and the sample evidence. Every item is marked as illustrative.
- `src/components/code/code-pane.tsx`: a server-rendered code view with hand-tokenised syntax highlighting, line numbers and an active-line highlight. This avoids an editor library on the homepage.
- `src/components/marketing/product-showcase.tsx`: a small client component where you pick a prediction, lock it in, apply the one-line fix, then see the expected and observed results side by side. A visible "Demonstration · sample output, not a live run" label stays on screen.

**Priority 4: supporting sections**, all in `src/components/marketing/`:

- `exercise-walkthrough.tsx`

**Priority 5: motion.**
- `src/components/motion/reveal.tsx`: entrance and scroll reveals that start visible without JavaScript and fully respect reduced motion.
- Hover and focus states in CSS.
- One trace-line animation in the loop section.

**Priority 6: checks.**
- `npm run lint`, `tsc --noEmit` and `npm run build`.
- With Playwright: screenshots at the §9 widths, plus a keyboard and reduced-motion pass.
- A written report of exactly what ran.

**New dependencies I'd add:** `motion` and `lucide-react`, plus `@playwright/test` as a dev dependency for the screenshot checks. I'd skip shadcn/Radix (nothing needs a dialog yet), CodeMirror (nothing is editable yet) and Shiki (hand-tokenised code is enough for a few fixed snippets).

## 5. Questions that would change the plan

1. **Where is the Trace backend?** If it lives in another repo or on another machine, point me to it, because the showcase and CTAs could then connect to real endpoints. If it doesn't exist yet, I'll build everything as clearly labelled sample data.
2. **Where should "Try a lesson" / "Try Trace" go for now?** I recommend linking to the interactive demo on the page (`#demo`) until a real lesson route exists. The alternative is a `/learn` page, but with no backend it would have to be an honest "coming soon".

4. **Can you share the Pinterest reference image?** It would make the ambient frame, navbar and hero match what you have in mind much more closely.

You are acting as a senior creative developer, product designer, art director, and frontend engineer working on **Trace**, a modern coding-learning product.

Your task is to design and implement an exceptional, production-quality frontend experience using Next.js.

**FIRST: Read the entire `CLAUDE.md` file in the project root.** Treat it as the authoritative reference for product understanding, design systems, typography, interactions, architecture, and quality standards.

## THE CREATIVE VISION

I want Trace to look like a premium, carefully art-directed SaaS product, with the refinement of modern Framer and Awwwards-quality websites.

The reference has several qualities I specifically want:

1. A beautiful, soft, warm gradient environment around the outside of the website.
2. A large, almost-white internal website canvas with carefully controlled rounded corners.
3. Floating, elegant navigation with small typography and a clear orange CTA.
4. A centered, oversized headline with dark typography and selective orange emphasis.
5. Exceptional whitespace, alignment, section proportions, and visual hierarchy.
6. A sophisticated product preview beneath the hero, surrounded by subtle ambient lighting and vertical light effects.
7. Premium feature cards with original visuals and carefully composed content.
8. Smooth, restrained motion that makes the interface feel alive without slowing it down.

Do not blindly copy the screenshot. Reinterpret its composition, design language, and artistic quality for Trace.

Trace must have its own identity.

## UNDERSTAND THE PRODUCT

Trace helps people who build software using AI actually understand the code that AI generates.

Our audience can use AI to create apps, APIs, and backend systems, but they may struggle to predict, debug, modify, and verify the generated code independently.

Trace's learning loop is:

Read real code → Predict behavior → Make a change → Verify the actual result → Revisit the concept later.

This learning experience must inform the visual design itself.

Our interface must not look like a generic online course, quiz platform, or dark terminal dashboard.

It should feel approachable to AI-assisted builders, but technically credible to experienced developers.

## FIRST TASK: REPOSITORY AUDIT

Before modifying anything:

Do not rebuild the entire project unnecessarily.

Do not replace functional product features with fake static screens.

## DESIGN SYSTEM

Build or refine a coherent, reusable visual system.

Use the updated color palette from `CLAUDE.md`.

The primary direction is:

- Almost-white background.
- Deep graphite typography.
- Warm orange accents.
- Soft peach, coral, and amber atmospheric gradients.
- Subtle gray borders.
- Gentle neutral surfaces.
- Dark editor surfaces only where they genuinely improve code readability.

Avoid excessive gradients, orange backgrounds everywhere, neon effects, generic glassmorphism, and randomly colored cards.

Use a refined modern sans-serif font, such as Geist Sans or Plus Jakarta Sans.

Make the hero typography a major design feature.

Pay close attention to letter spacing, line height, font weights, text wrapping, line lengths, and alignment.

The result should look intentionally typeset, not like default Tailwind typography.

The desktop experience should have generous outer margins, an inset main canvas, a compact floating header, a centered hero, and a large product preview.

The Trace homepage is built and working. Lint, TypeScript, the production build and 20 browser interaction checks all pass. I reviewed screenshots at 1440, 1280, 768, 390 and 320px and fixed what looked off.

Two caveats up front:
- **No screenshot arrived.** The Pinterest screenshot never came through (only text did), so the design follows the written description in CLAUDE.md. Send the image and I'll do a pass against it.
- **There's no backend.** Every code result on the page (HTTP responses, database rows, test results) is scripted sample output, and each is labelled that way. No FastAPI or SQLite exists anywhere on this machine, so nothing was connected or put at risk.

## What's on the page
1. **Frame and navigation:** a warm terracotta-to-cream gradient around a rounded off-white canvas. A floating white nav bar with centred links and an orange "Try Trace" button stays at the top and works on mobile.
2. **Hero:** your copy, with "understand" in orange. The headline sits on two lines on desktop and reflows cleanly on phones.
3. **Interactive lesson demo**, the signature piece. It takes about two minutes:
   - You see a FastAPI endpoint that returns 201 but never saves the note (a missing `db.commit()`).
   - You predict the outcome, and the prediction locks.
   - Revealing the sample result shows the response, an empty database query, and notes pinned to the two code lines responsible.
   - You type the fix directly into the code, with an optional hint and specific feedback on wrong answers.
   - It finishes with the evidence, an explanation, and a factual record such as "Prediction differed · Hint used · Fixed on try 2".
4. **Principles strip:** the four principles, separated by thin dividers.
5. **Three feature cards,** each with its own visual and an asymmetric layout:
   - a code path forking into two possible outcomes;
   - a one-line diff;
   - an expected-versus-observed table, which becomes a stacked list on phones.
6. **Learning loop:** the one dark section. An orange trace line connects the five steps and draws in as you scroll, with a return path from Revisit back to Read.
7. **Walkthrough:** a second complete exercise, a request with no title returning 422. It covers the request, the code, the prediction, the result, the fix and why it happened.
8. **Progress record:** an example timeline of a learner's work (first attempt, hint, revision, revisit, new scenario), plus what Trace records and what it won't show. There are no percentages or scores.
9. **Final CTA and footer.** All buttons and links go to real sections of the page.

## What actually works
- The whole lesson flow is interactive. Your fix is checked by comparing it with the expected line; nothing is executed, and the demo says so.
- The mobile menu closes with Escape, and focus moves into and back out of it correctly.
- There's a skip-to-content link for keyboard users, and screen readers hear each lesson step announced.
- With JavaScript turned off, or with reduced motion on, all content is still visible.

## Tests run
- **Lint, TypeScript and build:** all pass, and the page is generated as fully static.
- **Browser checks:** 20 of 20 pass. They cover the full demo flow, every in-page link, keyboard focus, the mobile menu, the no-JavaScript mode and reduced motion. The page has no sideways scrolling at any width and no console errors.
- **What I fixed after reviewing the screenshots:**
  - the headline wrapping to three lines;
  - a flat band above the hero;
  - boxy light columns behind the window;
  - a see-through nav;
  - the step rail getting cut off on phones;
  - a cramped table on mobile;
  - several alignment issues.
- **Not done:** I didn't test on real phones or in Safari, and I didn't run Lighthouse or an automated accessibility scan.

## Decisions you should know about
- **Two colours adjusted for readability.** The grey secondary text is a bit darker so body copy meets the WCAG AA contrast minimum (4.5:1). Orange *text* uses a slightly deeper orange, because `#FF8139` is too faint on white.
- **Orange buttons use dark text.** White text on that orange is hard to read (2.5:1); dark text is 7:1.
- **Dependencies:** added only `motion` and `lucide-react`. Playwright was used from a temporary folder and isn't in the project.
- **Removed** the unused starter images and favicon, and added a Trace icon.

## Files
- **Changed:** [globals.css](src/app/globals.css), [layout.tsx](src/app/layout.tsx), [page.tsx](src/app/page.tsx), `package.json` and `package-lock.json`.
- **New:**
  - [src/app/icon.svg](src/app/icon.svg)
  - [src/content/demo-lesson.ts](src/content/demo-lesson.ts): all the sample lesson content in one place
  - components under [src/components/](src/components/): `brand`, `layout`, `ui`, `code`, `marketing` and `motion`

Nothing is committed. Run `npm run dev` to see it.

**What Trace is:** a learning application that helps people become engineers who work effectively with AI coding agents. Learners practise reading, predicting, modifying, and verifying code, including code an agent might have written, so they can direct agents, review their output, and debug independently.

**What Trace is NOT:**
- It is not an AI agent, and it does not contain one.
- It is not an AI code generator, a chatbot, or an "AI tutor".
- Do not add chat interfaces, assistant panels, or "AI-powered" claims anywhere in the UI or copy.

## Error Type
Console Error

## Error Message
A tree hydrated but some attributes of the server rendered HTML didn't match the client properties. This won't be patched up. This can happen if a SSR-ed Client Component used:

- A server/client branch `if (typeof window !== 'undefined')`.
- Variable input such as `Date.now()` or `Math.random()` which changes each time it's called.
- Date formatting in a user's locale which doesn't match the server.
- External changing data without sending a snapshot of it along with the HTML.
- Invalid HTML tag nesting.

It can also happen if the client has a browser extension installed which messes with the HTML before React loaded.

https://react.dev/link/hydration-mismatch

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=7 session=ac502e61-757d-4998-a421-8b1428fcceb3]
timestamp: 2026-10-29T18:43:58.271Z
time: 2026-10-29 18:43
chars: 826

I fixed it. The error came from your ColorZilla browser extension, not from Trace's code. ColorZilla adds an attribute (`cz-shortcut-listen="true"`) to the page's `<body>` before React starts, so React sees HTML that doesn't match what the server sent. The automated browser in my earlier checks has no extensions, so they never hit it.

The change is one line in [layout.tsx](src/app/layout.tsx): `<body suppressHydrationWarning>`, plus a comment explaining why. It only ignores attribute differences on `<body>` itself, so real hydration bugs anywhere else will still be reported.

The TypeScript check and lint pass. Reload the dev server and the error should be gone. If you want to confirm the cause first, open the page in a private window with extensions disabled: the error won't appear there even without this change.

Animation pass for the Trace homepage. The visual design is approved and must not change. This task adds motion only, plus the shared components the motion needs. Re-read CLAUDE.md first (sections 7, 10, 11, 15), and update section 7 with the motion system below once it's implemented. Show me a short diff of that update.

Reference: I'm attaching screenshots from a premium Webflow template (Ostack). Take its motion quality and choreography as inspiration only. Don't copy its brand, content, stock photos, fake stats, or layout. Everything on screen stays Trace's: real code, real UI, sample data clearly labeled as sample.

## 0. Ground rules
- Library: Motion for React for ordinary reveals and hovers. GSAP + ScrollTrigger ONLY for the pinned/scrubbed sequences (section 2 hero-device sequence and section 5 steps). Check package.json first; install gsap only if absent, and load it dynamically (client components only, no GSAP in the initial bundle).
- No smooth-scroll hijacking libraries. Native scroll only.
- Animate transform and opacity. The only allowed use of filter: blur is on individual heading words (section 1) and the magnifier lens edge. Never blur large containers.
- Everything must work with JS disabled or before hydration: the final, readable state is the default CSS state. The hidden "from" state is applied only after JS runs (for example a `js-ready` class on <html>).
- prefers-reduced-motion: no scrub, no pinning, no blur, no movement. Show final states immediately, allow at most simple opacity fades under 200ms. Use gsap.matchMedia() for the GSAP parts.
- Never make learning content depend on animation. Lesson workspace routes get only microinteractions, no scroll choreography.
- Clean up all ScrollTriggers and timelines on unmount (gsap.context). Call ScrollTrigger.refresh() after fonts load. No layout shift (CLS must stay at or below 0.1).
- Keep Server Components as the default. Isolate animation in small client components, for example `<Reveal>`, `<SplitHeading>`, `<CodeWindow>`, `<FlowDiagram>`, `<DeviceSequence>`, `<StickySteps>`.
- Shared motion tokens in `lib/motion`: one easing curve for entrances (cubic-bezier(.22,1,.36,1)), one for hovers, durations from CLAUDE.md section 7. Use them everywhere. No ad-hoc values.
- Mobile (under 768px): no pinning, no 3D tilt. Use simple in-view reveals instead (described per item below).

## 1. Heading reveal (every section heading, SplitHeading)
- Split the heading into words (not letters). Each word starts at opacity 0, blur(12px), translateY(18px) and resolves to opacity 1, blur(0), translateY(0). Stagger 60-80ms, word duration about 700ms, entrance easing.
- Add a soft frosted feel while it resolves: a gradient veil mask that sweeps across the line in the same direction as the stagger, not a permanent glass panel. Once resolved, the heading is crisp with no leftover effect.
- Accent word (the orange "understand" or section accent) resolves last of its line with a slightly longer duration. If a small icon chip sits beside a word, it scales from 0.8 to 1 with a soft glow pulse once.
- Trigger once when the heading reaches about 85% of the viewport. Don't replay on scroll back.
- Accessibility: the full heading text is in the DOM as a single aria-label or visually-hidden copy; the animated word spans are aria-hidden. Heading order stays correct.
- Mobile: same effect with blur reduced to 6px and no veil.
- The hero headline keeps the sequence in CLAUDE.md section 7 (load-time, not scroll), using the same word reveal.

## 2. Hero device sequence (the signature scroll effect, DeviceSequence)
This replaces the static hero showcase entrance. Reference: the template's tilted device that flattens, then gets a background that grows behind it, then shrinks over that background. Build it as ONE pinned, scrubbed GSAP timeline (pin duration roughly 250-300vh of scroll, scrub about 0.6-1 for smoothing, no snapping).

The object: a premium device-style frame (graphite bezel, rounded 28-36px corners, small top notch details, thin inner bezel) containing the existing Trace lesson showcase UI. It must be the real showcase component, label "Interactive demo" retained, content crisp and legible in every phase.

Phases (percentages are of the timeline, tune by eye):
- Phase A, 0-25%: the frame starts tilted in perspective (parent perspective ~1400px; rotateX about 48-55deg, slight rotateZ about -4deg, scale about 1.08, translateY so it is cropped by the bottom of the viewport) on the near-white canvas with a soft fade at the bottom edge. It rises into view while the tilt begins to relax.
- Phase B, 25-50%: it flattens fully (rotateX 0, rotateZ 0, scale 1), centered, shadow deepens, bezel fully visible.
- Phase C, 50-75%: a large rounded background panel appears BEHIND the frame: it starts slightly narrower than the viewport with 40px radius, scale-in from 0.92 and opacity 0 to 1, peeking out on both sides of the frame. During this the bezel eases from graphite to a light neutral (#E9EAEE-ish) so the device feels like it's settling into the scene.
- Phase D, 75-100%: the panel expands to nearly full width of the canvas (radius easing from 40px to about 28px) while the device frame scales down to about 0.7 and stays centered, with the panel fully revealed around it. Do NOT shrink below about 0.7 so code stays readable.
- Background panel content (NO stock photos): a generated warm mesh gradient (peach, coral, amber, a hint of rose from our palette) with very faint execution-trace line work (thin SVG paths with small node dots, opacity under 0.25) and soft vertical light streaks consistent with the hero. Implement in CSS/SVG, no raster photography, no WebGL. A very slow drift of the gradient is allowed only when reduced-motion is off.
- Add subtle secondary parallax (translateY differences of a few percent) between the panel, frame, and streaks. Keep it subtle.
- Inside the frame, small purposeful in-window motion tied to scroll progress: step tracker advances Read to Predict as the frame flattens (this is demo-only state, labeled as the demo, not a verification result).
- Mobile and reduced motion: no pin, no 3D. The frame simply scales in (0.96 to 1) with opacity on view, and the background panel sits statically behind it.
- Verify the sticky pin does not cause jank or leave a big blank gap before/after, and that anchor links ("See how Trace works") still scroll correctly past the pinned region.

## 3. MacBook-style code windows + moving magnifier (CodeWindow)
- Build one reusable `<CodeWindow>` used for EVERY code excerpt on the marketing site: macOS-style window chrome (three traffic-light dots, centered or left file-name title like `app/routes/notes.py`, language badge), subtle shadow, 12-14px radius, line numbers, real syntax highlighting, code at 13-15px per CLAUDE.md. Light surface on the homepage, dark variant available for the lesson workspace. Content is real Python/FastAPI.
- Magnifier: a circular lens (about 120-160px, thin white rim with soft shadow and a small handle) that travels across the code window and pauses on specific lines to emphasize them. The lens is driven by an array of targets, for example `[{ line: 4, label: "validates input" }, { line: 9, label: "writes to DB" }]`.
- CRITICAL: text inside the lens must always be real and legible. Implement it as a second, scaled-up (about 1.6x) copy of the same code rendered inside the lens and clipped by the lens circle, positioned so the magnified region matches the lens center. Never blur, garble, or distort text. Use transform for lens movement. No canvas.
- The lens moves between targets with an eased glide (600-900ms, 400ms pause), highlights the targeted line with a soft orange band, and optionally shows a short caption chip that matches the line's meaning. Loop only while the window is in view; pause when off-screen (IntersectionObserver). Reduced motion: no moving lens; show the first target statically highlighted.
- Mobile: lens smaller (about 100px) or hidden if it hurts readability; line highlight still works.
- Place the magnified code window in the "Predict what happens" feature card and the exercise walkthrough, and use the plain CodeWindow (no lens) wherever else code appears.

## 4. Cards (Reveal + hover)
- Feature cards and other card grids enter on scroll with a staggered reveal: opacity 0 to 1, translateY 32px to 0, scale 0.98 to 1, 80-100ms stagger, about 600-700ms, entrance easing. Trigger once.
- Each card's inner visual gets a slightly delayed secondary reveal (for example diff lines appear, or evidence rows fade in one after another).
- Hover (desktop only): lift 4-6px, shadow softens up, the visual area scales 1.02 inside overflow hidden. 200ms. No tilt effects, no glow borders.
- The three feature cards keep their different surfaces (soft gray, white, warm gradient) so they don't look like clones.

## 5. Sticky steps section (StickySteps) for the learning loop
Reference: numbered steps on the left (01-05), the active one expands into a white card with its description, and a large visual on the right changes with the active step.
- Steps: READ, PREDICT, MODIFY, VERIFY, REVISIT, numbered 01-05. Left column: titles; the active step becomes an elevated white card (radius 16-20px, soft shadow) whose description expands smoothly (height via grid-template-rows 0fr to 1fr, plus opacity), while the others collapse to just title and number.
- Right column: a visual panel per step showing real Trace UI fragments (code excerpt for READ, prediction choices for PREDICT, a diff for MODIFY, an HTTP response and DB state evidence panel for VERIFY, a changed-scenario exercise for REVISIT). Transition between visuals: the incoming visual slides up 24px and fades in while the outgoing one fades and shifts up slightly, 500-600ms.
- Scroll-driven on desktop: pin the section and advance the active step with scroll progress (about 80-100vh per step), with the left progress indicator filling. Also make every step title clickable and keyboard accessible (buttons, aria-expanded, aria-controls, roving focus) to jump to that step.
- Mobile and reduced motion: no pin. Use a normal accordion with the step's visual shown inside the opened item.
- Sample content must be labeled as a demonstration where it isn't live.

## 6. Flow diagrams (FlowDiagram), sequential and smooth
Used in the learning-loop section and the exercise walkthrough. Sequence on view, played once:
1. First node card fades and rises in (about 500ms).
2. The connector draws from it (SVG stroke-dashoffset, 400-600ms), with a small dot travelling along the line.
3. Next node card appears as the connector arrives (slight scale 0.96 to 1).
4. Repeat. Branches draw out from a split point, with child cards staggered 120ms apart. The final successful card gets a check mark that scales in with a gentle spring.
- Example Trace flow content: `POST /notes` to `Pydantic validation` to `db.add + commit` to `201 Created`, with a branch for `422 Unprocessable` when `title` is missing. The labels are real concepts; any status or result shown is labeled as sample.
- Use the same cards, radii, and typography as the rest of the site. Connectors are thin 1.5px lines with arrowheads in the secondary text color; the active/traveling dot uses the orange accent. Static version (final state) renders correctly with no JS and with reduced motion.
- Optional replay on click of a small "Replay" text button; do not auto-loop.

## 7. Buttons, nav and microinteractions
- Navbar: gentle entrance on load (opacity and translateY -12px to 0, 500ms) and a very subtle shadow/blur change after scrolling past 40px. Mobile menu: smooth open (opacity and translate), focus trap, Escape to close, focus restored.
- Buttons: 150-200ms hover transitions (primary: slight brighten and 1px lift; secondary: border and background shift), clear focus-visible ring, pressed state, disabled and loading states already defined.
- Principles strip, pill labels, and footer get small, quiet fade reveals only.

## 8. Performance and quality bar
- Use will-change only on actively animating elements and remove it afterward. Avoid animating layout properties. Keep the pinned timeline's DOM lean.
- Lazy-load below-the-fold animation components (next/dynamic) and GSAP. Check that the initial JS bundle did not grow noticeably, and report the before/after size of the homepage route's first-load JS.
- Fonts must be loaded (next/font) before measuring SplitHeading line breaks; avoid reflow flicker when words split.
- No horizontal overflow at 320px at any scroll position. No scroll jank when entering or leaving the pinned sections.

## 9. Build order (commit after each stage)
1. Motion tokens, `<Reveal>`, `<SplitHeading>`, reduced-motion handling, and the CLAUDE.md section 7 update.
2. `<CodeWindow>` and the magnifier.
3. `<DeviceSequence>` (hero scroll sequence). Spend the most effort on this and iterate on feel until it's smooth.
4. Card reveals and hover.
5. `<FlowDiagram>`.
6. `<StickySteps>`.
7. Navbar, buttons, microinteractions, and mobile fallbacks.

## 10. Verification (don't skip, and don't claim anything you didn't run)
- Run `next build && next start` and test in the production build, not dev mode.
- With Playwright (or whatever browser tooling is available), scroll through the page and capture frames at 0%, 25%, 50%, 75%, and 100% of the device sequence, plus mid-reveal frames of a heading and a card grid. Review them against the reference for smoothness of states and correctness of each phase.
- Test at 1440x900, 1280x800, 768, 390x844, and 320. Check mobile fallbacks, reduced-motion behavior (emulate it), keyboard navigation through the steps and mobile menu, and that content is readable with JS disabled.
- Check that there is no horizontal overflow, no CLS regression, no console errors, and no leftover ScrollTrigger instances after navigating away and back.
- Run lint, the TypeScript check, existing tests, and confirm lesson and backend flows still work untouched.
- Report: files changed, which behaviors are real versus demo-only, what you verified and how, bundle-size impact, and anything that doesn't match the reference or still feels off. If you can't capture screenshots or measure frame rate, say so instead of claiming it.

The animation pass is finished, verified end to end, and committed. The homepage now has all seven pieces of motion you specified. Every automated check passes on the final production build: lint, TypeScript, the build itself, and about 110 browser checks. CLS is 0.0001, against a 0.1 budget.

Your Ostack screenshots didn't come through, so I worked from your written brief.

**What was built (on branch `feat/homepage-motion`)**

1. **Heading reveals.** Words go from blurred to crisp, with the orange accent word landing last and one soft sweep across the line. The hero headline does this from first paint, so it never waits for JavaScript to load.
2. **Code windows with a magnifier.** Every code excerpt is now a macOS-style window. A magnifying lens glides between key lines in the "Predict what happens" card and the walkthrough. Text inside the lens is a real enlarged copy of the code, never blurred. On phones the lens is hidden and the highlighted line plus a caption bar under the code do the job.
3. **Hero device sequence.** The lesson demo sits in a device frame that starts tilted, flattens as you scroll, settles onto a warm panel, and shrinks to 0.7 scale. The demo is fully usable inside it. "Try a lesson" lands at the point where the device is flat.
4. **Cards.** Staggered entrances, then a quick reveal of their contents, and a 4px lift on hover.
5. **Flow diagrams.** A request is traced through the code: lines draw with a moving dot, branches split, and the success card gets a check. There's a Replay button. Results are labelled "Sample run".
6. **Sticky steps for the learning loop.** Read, Predict, Modify, Verify, Revisit advance as you scroll on desktop, and the steps work fully by keyboard. Phones get an accordion. With JavaScript off, every step is shown.
7. **Nav, menu and buttons.** The nav shadow deepens on scroll. The mobile menu animates, keeps keyboard focus inside it while open, and closes on Escape. Buttons lift slightly on hover.

**Real versus demo:** all motion and interaction is real. Every lesson result, response, database row and test outcome is scripted sample data, and it's labelled that way. The step tracker holding on "Read" until the device flattens is display-only; it doesn't change lesson state.

**Problems found during testing, and fixed**
- **Layout shift.** GSAP's built-in pinning caused a large layout shift (CLS 0.376 at desktop width) and knocked keyboard focus out of the lesson's code box. I replaced it with CSS sticky positioning and kept GSAP only for the scroll-linked animation. CLS dropped to 0.0001.
- **Lesson cut off on desktop.** The demo grows as you advance. Its tallest step, Verify, ran off-screen inside the device. The device now rescales to fit and stays centred.
- **Invisible cards and diagram nodes.** In one animation pattern, cards and diagram nodes stayed invisible until the end of their reveal.
- **Silent failures.** If the scroll animation failed to load, the page now reports why instead of failing silently.

**How it was verified**
- **Browser checks:** about 110 automated checks in Edge on production builds, at 1440, 1280, 768, 390 and 320px.
- **Coverage:** reduced motion, JavaScript disabled, keyboard use, the full lesson played inside the device, anchor links, and no sideways scrolling at any width. I also looked at frames from each part of the sequence.
- **Cleanup:** in your running dev server (port 3000, left untouched), exactly 2 scroll animations stay live after React's strict-mode remount and a full scroll.
- **Bundle size:** first-load JavaScript went from 226.3 KB to 242.9 KB gzip (+16.6 KB). GSAP adds 43.8 KB gzip, loaded only on desktop with motion allowed.

**Where it differs from your brief**
- **Pinning:** the scroll sections use CSS sticky instead of GSAP's pin, which was the layout-shift fix above.
- **Sticky steps:** they scroll-drive from 1024px rather than 768px. Tablets get the accordion, because two columns were too cramped.
- **Lens timing:** the lens rests 1.3 seconds on each line instead of 0.4, so its caption can be read.
- **Panel corners:** the background panel's corners don't animate from 40px to 28px, to avoid repainting a large blurred area on every frame.
- **Card surfaces:** the three cards keep their approved warm surfaces rather than gray, white and warm, since the visual design was frozen.
- **Code in its original form:** the lesson demo's own code panel and the 422 response panel aren't in the new window style, so working lesson logic stays untouched.

**Limits:** frame timing was measured in a headless browser without a real graphics card (about 56fps on average, 2 frames over 50ms), so treat it as a rough guide. I haven't tested Safari or real phones, or run Lighthouse.

The CLAUDE.md §7 changes are shown in the diff above. In short, it now documents the motion tokens, sticky positioning with the reason for it, the explicit end-keyframe rule, and each component's behaviour. Nothing has been pushed to GitHub.

Set `pinSpacing` so the next section starts directly after the last step with just our normal section spacing token, with no extra blank area before or after.

The learning-loop section is fixed and now matches the reference's layout. The cause of the huge empty area was a bug in my earlier sticky implementation, not a styling issue. Lint and TypeScript pass, and the measurements below come from production builds. Nothing is committed or pushed.

**Root cause.** None of the suspects on your list were present: no `min-h-screen`, no oversized `end`, no leftover pin spacer, and the section padding wasn't stacking. Instead, the steps block never stuck at all:
- I'd given its scroll track the extra length as `padding-bottom` (3,825px).
- A sticky element can only move within its parent's content box, and padding isn't part of that box, so the block had no room to stick.
- It scrolled away immediately, and the 3,825px of empty padding scrolled past behind it. Measured 600px into the section, the block was at −600px with 1,128px of blank viewport below.
- The step still changed because ScrollTrigger reads scroll position, not where the block is. My earlier test only checked the computed style said "sticky", so it missed this.

**The "Explain" button isn't in Trace's code.** The search confirms nothing in the repo renders it. A browser extension (an AI code-explainer) injects it, together with its copy icon, into code blocks, so disabling that extension, or turning it off for this site, removes it. I added a real copy button inside our code window's title bar. It copies the code and shows "Copied", and it can't overlap anything.

**What changed**
- **Light section** on the standard container: pill label, word-reveal heading, then the two-column grid.
- **Equal columns:** both are `max(520px, min(78vh, 680px))` tall.
  - Left: titles with numbers at the right edge, the active step an elevated white card, and a progress line.
  - Right: one tall warm mesh panel that never changes size, with the step's white card centred inside it.
- **Scroll track:** exactly the grid height plus 70vh per step (350vh). The block sticks vertically centred.
- **Step visuals:** the code window, the prediction options, a before/after diff, an expected-versus-observed table, and the Revisit exercise with "Hints used: 0". Sample data is labelled. Long code lines are wrapped so every card fits without scrolling.

**Measured**

| | 1440×900 | 1280×800 | 1024×768 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Block / left column / right panel | 680 / 680 / 680px | 624 / 624 / 624px | 599 / 599 / 599px |
| Block position while stuck | constant at 134px | constant at 112px | constant at 109px |
| Empty space below the block while stuck | 86px | 64px | 60px |
| Gap to the next content (section spacing token) | 144px (144px) | 128px (128px) | 102px (102px) |
| Cards fit without scrolling | all 5 | all 5 | all 5 |

- **At every size:** scrolling advances Read → Predict → Verify → Revisit, the block releases cleanly in both directions, and there's no horizontal overflow and no console errors.
- **Keyboard:** Enter on a step jumps to it, and the arrow keys move between steps.
- **768 and 390:** these get the accordion with the visual inside the open item. The Verify table restacks on phones so it fits.
- **No JavaScript:** all five steps show as a readable list.
- **Back/forward navigation:** returning to the page leaves no pin spacers and exactly one scroll trigger.
- **Rest of the page:** the hero sequence, lesson demo, flows and reveals all still pass. Whole-page CLS is 0.009 at desktop and 0 on phones.
- **Screenshots:** I captured the six scroll positions at each desktop size and the accordions at 768 and 390, and reviewed the key frames myself.

**Still different from your spec or the reference**
- **Layout shift while switching steps.** It measures 0.009 at 1440, 0.011 at 1280 and 0.020 at 1024 per full pass, which is under the 0.1 budget but not zero. It comes from the active card expanding and moving the rows below it, which is inherent to this accordion layout. Removing it would need fixed-height row slots, and the descriptions don't fit those at 1024.
- **Sticky, not GSAP pin.** I kept CSS sticky instead of GSAP `pin`/`pinSpacing`. The scroll behaviour is the same, and GSAP's pin caused the 0.376 CLS and the focus loss last round.
- **Proportions at 1024–1279px.** The columns are roughly 4.6/7.4 instead of 5/7, and the card fills 94% of the panel instead of 85%, so the code fits without scrolling. From 1280px it's 5/7 at 85%.

**Files changed:** [learning-loop.tsx](src/components/marketing/learning-loop.tsx), [sticky-steps.tsx](src/components/marketing/sticky-steps.tsx), [globals.css](src/app/globals.css), [code-window.tsx](src/components/code/code-window.tsx), the new [copy-code.tsx](src/components/code/copy-code.tsx), and the CLAUDE.md §7 entry for this section.

## Changes
**Spacing and structure**
- Rows size to their content. Collapsed row height about 64-72px, with an 8-12px gap between rows. Remove any flex-grow, justify-between, or fixed-height rules applied at this breakpoint. The whole list should be compact, with no empty gaps.
- Open row: white card (radius 18px, 1px border, soft shadow) containing the icon, title, number, the description, and the step's visual below the description.

**Icons (small, consistent, not decorative blobs)**
- One Lucide icon per step, stroke width 1.75, 18-20px, inside a 36-40px rounded-12px chip with a 1px border:
  - READ: file-code (or book-open)
  - PREDICT: crosshair (or help-circle)
  - MODIFY: pencil-line (or diff)
  - VERIFY: shield-check (or check-circle-2)
  - REVISIT: rotate-ccw (or repeat)
- Chip states: inactive = surface-muted background with secondary-text icon; active = primary-soft background with a primary-colored icon and a primary-tinted border; completed (steps before the active one) = icon swaps to a small check, with success-neutral styling (do not use orange to mean success, per CLAUDE.md).
- Layout of each row: icon chip at left, title next to it, step number (01-05) at the far right in the mono font. Inactive rows also show a one-line muted teaser under the title (about 12 words, for example for PREDICT: "Commit to an outcome before you run anything."). The teaser collapses away when the row opens, because the full description takes over.

**Progress line ("follow the signal")**
- A thin vertical line (2px) runs through the center of the icon chips on the left, with a light gray track.
- The orange fill extends only from the first chip down to the currently active chip, animating its height (use transform: scaleY with transform-origin top, 400ms, entrance easing). Completed chips sit on the filled part; later chips sit on the gray part.
- A small dot or glow at the end of the fill marks the active position. Respect reduced motion (no animation, instant state).

**Visual inside the open step**
- Below the description, show a compact version of the step's visual: READ shows a CodeWindow with 4-6 lines; PREDICT shows the question with 2-3 option rows; MODIFY shows a small before/after diff; VERIFY shows expected vs observed with HTTP status and DB state; REVISIT shows the changed-scenario exercise with a "Hints used: 0" evidence line. Use the same components as the desktop panel, scaled for narrow width. Label any sample data as sample. Code must stay at least 12px with horizontal scroll if needed, and the layout must not overflow.
- Open and close animation: grid-template-rows 0fr to 1fr plus opacity, about 350ms. The visual fades and rises 12px about 100ms after the card opens. Only one step is open at a time. Opening another closes the current one. Scroll the newly opened row into view if it would end up off-screen (smooth, not jarring, and not under any sticky header).

**Behavior and accessibility**
- Each row header is a real button with aria-expanded and aria-controls, a visible focus ring, and a minimum 44px tap target. Arrow keys move between rows. Enter/Space toggles.
- Auto-advance on scroll is not needed at this breakpoint. It's tap-driven.
- Works with JS disabled (all five steps readable as a stacked list), and with reduced motion (no movement, instant state change).
- Text contrast AA for inactive rows (current inactive gray is borderline, so darken the title slightly).

## Verification (don't claim anything you didn't run)
- Check on a production build at 390x844, 360x800, 320, and 768. Capture the closed state with step 1 open, and states with step 3 and step 5 open. Confirm the progress line ends exactly at the active chip each time.
- Measure that the gap between collapsed rows is at most 12px, and confirm no horizontal overflow and no layout jump when switching steps.
- Run lint and the TypeScript check. Report files changed, what you measured, and what still differs from this description.
