- Tasks: the line `"priority": task.priority,` carries an inline control ("Nest this field") that rewrites the payload in place into the nested shape (`"metadata": { "priority": task.priority }`), with a toggle to undo back to flat. The code reflows with a short transition, and the diff highlight shows the changes. The sequencing from the backend contract stays: the learner must first run the original FLAT request (mandatory failure evidence) before the nested edit unlocks.
- Under the code, one primary action: "Check my change". It performs the existing real flow (save the attempt with a fresh idempotency key, then verify, reusing keys on uncertain retries exactly as the existing client does) and displays the real evidence. Keep an optional "What changed and why?" textarea (maps to `explanation`, max 2000 chars). Don't change the request or verification logic, only the UI that feeds it.
- A short honest note near the control: "You edit this line inside the code. Trace checks a fixed set of changes against the real server." Show a "Reset" button that restores the original line.
- Accessibility: the chip is a real button with an accessible name ("Change field name, currently body"), a keyboard-operable popover (arrow keys, Enter, Escape), and focus restored on close. Respect reduced motion. Minimum 44px tap target on mobile.
- Verification behaves exactly as before: failed means the mapping failed (edit again), `error` is infrastructure ("Check again"), completion appears only from server `completed`.

## Task 3: Tutor panel refinement (preserve the mockup's design)
- Keep the look and the structure in the screenshot. Add: a context line at the top that shows what the tutor sees ("Lesson: The Nested Payload Problem. Step: Run the original request"), updating as the learner moves through steps. Send lesson id, step, and the real evidence already on the page as context to the adapter (never anything private beyond that).
- Actions: "Explain this concept" (mock), "Give me a hint" (REAL fixture endpoint, three slots shown from `summary.hint_request_count` and `hints_delivered`), "Why did this fail?" (appears only after a real failed verification and receives that run's checks as context; mock explanation), and the free-text question box (mock, length limit and counter).
- Behaviors: the final worked explanation is a deliberate last step with a confirm. The tutor never says a learner is right or finished. AI output uses the "AI explanation (demo)" label and is visually distinct from the verification evidence. Chat history is kept in memory for the page session. Include loading, streaming (if the adapter supports it), error with retry, rate-limited, unavailable, guest-not-allowed, and empty states, `aria-live` for new answers, focus management for the drawer, and the privacy line.
- The mock content must be correct and grounded in the REAL lessons (Notes: key name mismatch and forbidden extra fields lead to 422 and no row; Tasks: `priority` must be inside `metadata`), and must follow the hint ladder and never reveal the full solution early.

## Task 4: Lesson library prepared for future AI-generated lessons
In the hub (`/learn`), restructure into a lesson library:
- Section "Trace lessons": the two real lessons (existing cards, status and locking from the API).
- Section "Your lessons": an honest empty state: "Custom lessons you create will appear here." No fake lessons.
- A "Create a custom lesson" card with a designed request form UI: topic (text, with example placeholder "I want to learn FastAPI authentication from beginner to intermediate with practical exercises"), level selector (beginner to intermediate), a preference for practical exercises, and a clear preview of what a generated lesson will contain (explanations, coding challenges, verification criteria) shown as a static illustration labeled "Preview of the format". The submit button is DISABLED with the visible message "Coming soon: custom lessons are being built" and no fake loading, no fake output, and no mock generated lesson. Don't call any endpoint.
- Architecture only: define a small `LessonSource` abstraction (catalog from the existing static registry plus real journey data now; generated source is a stub that reports "unavailable") so a future real source slots in. Add PROPOSED types for lesson-creation requests and generated lesson structure in `src/lib/ai/contracts.ts` with the "PROPOSED, not a backend contract" header. Nothing calls them.
- Make sure Python/FastAPI is the only scope shown in the form examples.

## Task 5: Docs for my teammate
Create or update `docs/frontend-api-requests.md` with proposed contracts for BOTH capabilities (and keep any existing content):
1. Contextual tutor: ask, explain concept, why-did-this-fail (should accept a stored verification run id so the backend uses stored evidence, never learner-supplied evidence), hint-level ownership, guest access, solution-leak policy, streaming or not, rate limits, error codes, and no-store behavior.
2. AI lesson creation: request creation (topic, level, practical-exercise preference), asynchronous status and retrieval (queued, generating, ready, failed, needs-review), the structure of a generated lesson (explanation sections, coding challenges, verification criteria), how generated coding challenges get VERIFIED (the current verification only supports the two hand-built runtimes), review and approval, quotas and cost caps, safety, list endpoint for a learner's custom lessons, and how generated lessons appear in the journey. Mark all as PROPOSED, "needs backend agreement".
3. A short adapter swap checklist (which files change when real endpoints arrive).
4. Update CLAUDE.md: the tutor is the primary AI experience, AI lesson creation is a separate future feature, mocks must be labeled, credentials never touch the frontend. Show me the diff.

## Tests
- Vitest: code windows render all lines without horizontal overflow for both real lesson code strings at several widths (assert no scrollWidth > clientWidth for normal lines), inline edit chips produce exactly the allowed `field_name` values and never anything else, Tasks nested edit stays locked until the real flat run exists, "Check my change" sends the same request shapes and idempotency behavior as before, tutor panel states and the context line, the "Why did this fail?" visibility rule, hint slots from summary counts, the disabled custom-lesson form never triggers a network call, and the "Your lessons" empty state.
- Playwright (desktop and Pixel 7) against the repo's test backend (temporary migrated and seeded DB): Notes full journey using the in-code edit; Tasks full journey (flat failure first, then nested edit and pass); tutor panel collapse and drawer; keyboard-only path through the in-code edit; hub shows the library sections and the disabled custom-lesson card; no page errors; no horizontal scrollbar on code in either lesson at 1280, 1024, 768, 390.
- Keep all existing tests passing, and update only tests whose intended behavior changed. Don't weaken assertions.

## Verify (don't claim what you didn't run)
Run a production build and capture screenshots at 1440x900, 1280x800, 1024x768, 768, 390x844, and 320 for: both lessons at the start, the in-code edit open, a failed run, a completed run, the tutor panel expanded and collapsed, the mobile tutor drawer, and the library hub. Inspect them yourself and fix overlap, wrapping, overflow, and contrast issues. Run lint, typecheck, Vitest, the `npm run api:types` drift check, and Playwright. Report each command with its real result.

## Final report (under 40 lines)
What changed per task, files created and changed, what is real versus mock and where labels appear, test results, anything unverified, and exact questions for my teammate. Don't commit and don't push.

The lesson page and library refinement is done, and signed-in users now get the real lessons while guests only get the demos. Every check passed. Nothing is committed or pushed, and I didn't touch backend code, env files, `openapi.json` or `.claude-logs/*`.

**Sign-in rule (new)**
- **Guests on a lesson page** see "Sign in to start this lesson", with Sign in, Create account and "Try the demo lesson" (the homepage demo). No learner, session or lesson request is made, and the tutor isn't shown.
- **Guests on `/learn`** see the two Trace lessons (title, concept, length) with "Sign in to start" buttons, plus the empty "Your lessons" section and the custom-lesson preview. No journey call is made.
- **Guests on `/today`** keep the demo steps. The real lesson step becomes "Sign in to start".
- **After sign-in** from a lesson page, you land back on that lesson. Guest history from before this change can still be claimed after sign-in.
- **Other changes:** the "Save progress" link on lesson pages is now "Sign in". The expired-sign-in message no longer offers "continue as a guest".
- **This is a front-end rule only.** The backend still accepts guest cookies.

**Lesson page and library (Tasks 1–5)**
- **Editing:** the change step is an edit inside the code. Notes has a chip on the key with `body` / `content`; Tasks has "Nest this field" / "Undo nesting", locked until the original request has run. Both show a before/after diff and send only the allowed values.
- **Layout:** code wraps instead of scrolling sideways, each code block appears once, and text stays at least 13px.
- **Tutor:** a collapsible side panel from 1280px, a right-edge drawer at 1024–1279px, and a bottom drawer below that. A line at the top shows the lesson and current step, and chat history survives collapsing.
- **Library:** `/learn` has "Trace lessons", an empty "Your lessons", and a disabled "Create a custom lesson" form that makes no network calls.
- **Docs:** [frontend-api-requests.md](docs/frontend-api-requests.md) now covers the tutor, AI lesson creation (proposed only), the sign-in rule and the swap-checklist steps. [CLAUDE.md](CLAUDE.md) has three new bullets in its AI section; you can see the diff with `git diff CLAUDE.md`.

**What's real and what's a demo:** verification, hints ("Fixture guidance"), attempts and progress are real. Tutor replies are demo responses with a "Demo responses" badge and an "AI explanation (demo)" label. The `/today` demo steps are labelled "Demo exercise", and custom lessons are UI only.

**Files:** new ones are [sign-in-required.tsx](src/components/account/sign-in-required.tsx), [guest-library.tsx](src/components/learning/hub/guest-library.tsx), [custom-lessons.tsx](src/components/learning/hub/custom-lessons.tsx), [editable-request.tsx](src/components/learning/editable-request.tsx), [edits.ts](src/lib/learning/edits.ts), [lesson-source.ts](src/lib/learning/lesson-source.ts) and the test helper [account.ts](tests/e2e/helpers/account.ts). I also changed the workspace, forms, tutor panel, code rows, hub, today path, the gate component, `client.ts`, `contracts.ts` and `globals.css`.

**Test changes:** the browser tests now sign up before opening a lesson; their existing checks are unchanged. The two guest-claim tests create their guest history through the real API, because the UI no longer can. Three tests were rewritten for the new rule: the guest hub and `/today` link in the browser suite, and the guest hub unit test (it now checks that guests make no learner request). The timed transfer test now resets the backend's test clock afterwards. Without that, signed-in tokens looked expired to the backend in later tests.

**Checks run**

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Lint, `tsc --noEmit` | clean |
| Vitest | 134 of 134 pass |
| `api:types` drift | none (line endings only) |
| Production build | succeeded |
| Playwright (desktop and Pixel 7, repo test backend) | 56 passed, 4 skipped on purpose (desktop-only checks on the phone run) |
| Screenshots at 1440, 1280, 1024, 768, 390, 320 (including the guest screens) | 74 automatic checks pass; I looked at a sample myself |

**Not checked:** the unit tests can't measure real layout, so "no sideways code scrolling" is measured only in the browser tests.

**Questions for Soorej**
1. Should the backend also refuse new guest learners and guest lesson sessions?
2. Can guests use the tutor?
3. Can the tutor's "ask" request take a `step` field?
4. Should viewing the worked explanation be recorded as a learning event?
5. For custom lessons: is human review required, what are the quotas, and do they appear in the journey?

3. MOBILE ONLY: the hint request returns 401 in `ai-surfaces.spec.ts:57` (expected 201 from `POST .../hints`), and "Hint 1 · fixture guidance" never appears in `api-contract.spec.ts:18` and `learning.spec.ts:7`. The same tests pass on desktop, and earlier steps on mobile (prediction, attempt, verify) succeeded.

Steps:
1. Did the recent guest-gating change (a test named "guests only get demos: lessons ask for sign-in") stop creating guest identities on page load? Fix the test setup (for example bootstrap the guest explicitly with the right headers, or use the repo's existing auth fixture) only if the product behavior is intended.

Check the mobile tutor drawer and the in-lesson guidance component (is the hint request sent through the shared API client with `credentials: "include"` and the same idempotency and auth logic? is it a duplicate component or a second client? does the mobile layout mount a different instance without the identity or bearer context? does the 401-refresh-then-resend logic misfire for guests? is the guest cookie lost between steps on mobile, for example because of a different base URL or a navigation?). All 7 CI failures came from stale tests and test setup, not app bugs. The fixes are uncommitted in your working tree (CI ran the snapshot committed as `9042f22`), and the full suite now passes locally with CI settings.

**What reproduced:** running your committed specs with `CI=true`, retries 1, 1 worker and both projects gave the same 7 failures (49 passed, 4 skipped, 401s included). Two things differ from CI: I skipped `npm ci` (it would reinstall `node_modules` under your dev server on :3000), and the test backend ran on :8010 because your backend uses :8000.

**Root causes**
1. **legacy-version (stale test):** the test calls `POST /sessions` itself, relying on the guest cookie the lesson page used to create. The test now signs in, and signed-in pages send a Bearer token instead (cookie host `127.0.0.1` is consistent). The test now reuses the page's own Bearer header. Fixed in [legacy-version.spec.ts](tests/e2e/legacy-version.spec.ts) and [account.ts](tests/e2e/helpers/account.ts).
2. **lessons-hub (stale test):** guests no longer load the journey, so "0 of 2 lessons completed" is gone for guests by design; it still shows for signed-in learners. The guest test now checks the sign-in prompt and that the count is absent. A new signed-in test keeps the original "0 of 2" assertion. Fixed in [lessons-hub.spec.ts](tests/e2e/lessons-hub.spec.ts).
3. **Mobile-only hint 401 (test isolation):** the timed transfer test moves the backend's test clock to 2026-11-09 and never resets it. Desktop runs every spec first, so the transfer test finishes before any mobile test starts. Every signed-in mobile test after it gets 401 on hints, because the backend compares token expiry to that clock. It isn't the mobile layout or a second client: hints use the one shared client, and both projects behave the same.
   - **Evidence:** a probe gave 201 on real time and 401 `unauthenticated` with the clock left behind, on both projects, with the Bearer present each time.
   - **Fix:** [transfer-retrieval.spec.ts](tests/e2e/transfer-retrieval.spec.ts) now restores real time after it runs, and the sign-up helper clears any leftover clock.

**Guest access (please decide):** right now guests are demo-only. On the real Notes and Tasks lessons they get a "Sign in to start" screen and no learner request is made; they keep the homepage demo and the `/today` demo steps. I made this change because your previous message asked for it ("when the user is signed in then they see the lessons and otherwise they will only work on the demos"). It conflicts with the earlier "guest learning stays available" decision, and the backend still supports guests. The passing test "guests only get demos" asserts this rule, so I kept it. Reverting it means removing the `signIn` option from the gate in [workspace.tsx](src/components/learning/workspace.tsx) plus the guest branches in the hub and `/today`.

**Other changes:** the 401 message no longer says "continue as a guest" ([client.ts](src/lib/api/client.ts)), and I removed two unused imports in [lessons-hub.tsx](src/components/learning/hub/lessons-hub.tsx).

**Results (local, current tree)**
- Previously failing specs plus the transfer test, both projects: 33 passed, 3 skipped, 0 failed, 0 flaky.
- Full Playwright suite: 58 passed, 4 skipped (desktop-only tests on the phone project), 0 failed.
- `eslint`: clean. `npm run typecheck`: passes. Vitest: 134 of 134. `api:types`: no drift.

I can't say CI will pass until it runs these changes.

**One urgent item:** your Gemini key is in `backend/.env.gemini.example`, which Git tracks (that's the local modification Git shows). It hasn't been committed, stashed, logged or built into the frontend. Don't run `git add` until it's moved: put that line in `backend/.env.gemini` (Git ignores it), then run `git checkout -- backend/.env.gemini.example backend/ai.env.example`. That also restores the example file you deleted.

| Lesson tutor: ask, concept, why-it-failed | Real, one endpoint: `POST /sessions/{id}/tutor/ask` with `{question}` (1–400 chars). Returns `answered`, `declined` or `unavailable`. Gemini only picks approved text. The server loads the run itself; no run id is accepted. |

**Why demos still show, ranked**
1. `NEXT_PUBLIC_AI_MODE` is unset in `.env.local`, so the adapter defaults to mock. The real tutor and support clients are already wired.
2. Your backend (PID 31416) was started with plain `uvicorn`. No `TRACE_LLM_*` variable is set in any environment scope, and those settings are read only from the process environment, never from `backend/.env`. So AI mode is `disabled` and both endpoints would reply `unavailable`.

4. Real mode on its own would break things:

   - The interest picker would show an error in real mode.
   - Guests get 401 from Trace Help.

**Already fine:**
- Generated types match `openapi.json` (no drift).
- The running server isn't stale; it serves all 24 paths.
- Migrations are at head (`0007_tutor_reservations`).
- `google-genai` 2.34.0 is locked and installed, so no `uv sync` is needed.
- There's no seed step.
- No key or provider SDK appears in the frontend source, `.env.local` or the `.next` build output.

**Live check:** not run. The backend can't see the key and the `TRACE_LLM_*` variables aren't set.

**Free tier**
- **Limits in his code:**
  - 5 requests per minute per account, per feature.
  - 1 request at a time per account, 2 globally.
  - 20 calls per process (resets on restart).
  - 10-second provider timeout, 20-second request deadline, no retries.
- **What the UI shows:** a 429 shows "That's a lot of questions…", and `unavailable` shows the unavailable state.
- **Logging:** I found no logging of questions or answers, and the start script turns off the access log. This is from searching the code, not an audit.
- **Privacy:** under Google's terms for unpaid use, free-tier content may be used to improve Google's products and may be read by human reviewers. Check Google's current terms before launch. Suggested UI line: "Questions go to Google's Gemini (free tier). Google may use them to improve its products. Don't include personal details, passwords or private code."

**Integration plan (needs your approval)**

- Tie `DemoLabel` to each client's mode, so the labels on the tutor and Trace Help go away but stay on `/today` and interests.

TRACE — CLAUDE.md
Product, Creative Direction, Design System & Engineering Standards
This document is the single source of truth for designing and developing Trace. Read it fully before making architectural, visual, or functional changes.

Primary objective: Build an outstanding, premium, highly polished web application with the visual refinement of a leading creative development studio, while maintaining Trace's genuine learning functionality.

Design references:

Primary visual reference: The warm orange and white SaaS landing page supplied by the project owner (Pinterest reference).
Creative quality reference: https://www.karma.business/
Do not copy either reference literally. Extract their design principles and apply them to Trace's own identity.

1. PRODUCT UNDERSTANDING
What is Trace?
Trace is a coding-learning platform for people who build software using AI but want to understand the resulting code well enough to work independently.

The problem:

Someone uses AI to build a FastAPI backend, an endpoint, or a database integration.

The application works, but when the API fails, a database write does not happen, or a bug appears, the builder might not understand the code's behavior.

They can generate more code with AI, but cannot confidently reason about or modify the existing implementation themselves.

Trace exists to close this gap.

Core proposition
You built it. Now understand it.

Trace helps AI-assisted builders learn to:

Read and understand realistic code.
Predict how code will behave.
Identify bugs and misconceptions.
Make focused code changes.
Verify actual execution results.
Apply previously learned concepts independently.
Trace is not a conventional coding quiz platform, AI code generator, or passive tutorial application.

It is a practice environment focused on understanding real program behavior.

The learning loop
The core workflow is:

READ → PREDICT → MODIFY → VERIFY → REVISIT

READ
The learner reads a small piece of realistic application code.

PREDICT
The learner predicts the behavior before seeing the result.

MODIFY
The learner changes an expression or a small section of code.

VERIFY
Trace checks the actual behavior using execution results, API responses, or database state.

REVISIT
Trace presents a related but changed exercise later, helping assess whether the learner retained the underlying concept.

Sessions should typically take approximately 5–10 minutes.

The interface must make these steps immediately understandable.

Evidence over invented scores
Trace tracks:

Initial predictions.
Learner attempts.
Hint usage.
Code revisions.
Verification outcomes.
Follow-up exercise results.
Do not invent IQ scores, mastery percentages, productivity metrics, or unverified learning claims.

Use evidence such as:

"Correct on first attempt."

"Hint used before revision."

"API returned HTTP 422."

"Database record not created."

"Revisit completed without hints."

The difference between code evidence and learning evidence is fundamental to the product.

2. DESIGN PHILOSOPHY
Creative vision
Trace should look like a modern premium technology product designed by an experienced art director and creative frontend engineer.

The visual target combines:

Premium editorial typography.
Carefully controlled whitespace.
Warm atmospheric gradients.
Refined nearly white surfaces.
High-quality technical product demonstrations.
Purposeful motion.
Elegant component composition.
Clear information architecture.
Precise alignment.
Strong visual storytelling.
Every section must appear intentionally designed.

The product should feel sophisticated, intelligent, approachable, and technically credible.

Design concept
FOLLOW THE SIGNAL

Trace follows a signal through code:

Input → Execution → Outcome → Understanding.

Represent this idea with:

Fine connecting lines.
Subtle active-line highlights.
Trace paths.
Numbered learning stages.
Precise technical labels.
Carefully animated sequence indicators.
Clear relationships between code and verified output.
Avoid turning the website into an overly futuristic or cyberpunk dashboard.

The design should remain calm and readable.

What the reference image teaches us
The supplied Pinterest reference demonstrates a particularly effective composition.

Outer ambient frame
A warm gradient surrounds the main website.

The gradient includes muted:

Terracotta.
Coral.
Peach.
Amber.
Warm cream.
A large off-white inner canvas sits above this environment.

The framing gives the page depth without overwhelming the content.

Navigation
The navbar is compact, floating, clean, and visually lightweight.

Its interior background is white.

The typography is small but sharp.

The primary CTA uses warm orange.

Hero composition
The headline sits in the visual center.

The main text is deep graphite.

One important phrase is highlighted orange.

The copy has carefully measured line lengths, spacing, and line breaks.

Product demonstration
A large application preview appears below the hero.

Soft ambient lighting and subtle vertical light treatments create visual depth behind it.

For Trace, this preview must depict a realistic interactive learning experience, not a banking dashboard.

Supporting sections
Sections are composed with strong hierarchy and consistent spacing.

Feature cards contain original, purpose-made visuals.

The design uses repetition where useful, but not identical cards everywhere.

Do not reproduce the original brand, copy, icons, logos, or product screenshots.

3. VISUAL DESIGN SYSTEM
Color palette
Use CSS variables for all colors.

:root {
  --ambient-base: #C88D81;
  --background: #FAFAFC;
  --surface: #FFFFFF;
  --surface-muted: #F4F5F7;

  --foreground: #151923;
  --text-secondary: #717784;
  --text-tertiary: #9196A1;

  --border: #E5E7EC;

  --primary: #FF8139;
  --primary-hover: #E96E29;
  --primary-soft: #FFF0E6;

  --editor-background: #1A1E27;
  --editor-foreground: #E8EAF0;

  --success: #227953;
  --error: #BD4B4B;
  --warning: #A46D2B;
}
Color rules
Most of the interface must remain nearly white.
Graphite should be used for primary text.
Orange is a signature accent, not the color of every element.
Use orange for important actions and selective highlighted text.
Ambient gradients should be atmospheric rather than dominant.
Semantic success and error colors must remain distinguishable.
Do not use orange to indicate successful verification.
Maintain WCAG-compliant text contrast.
Avoid:

Generic purple-blue AI gradients.
Neon colors.
Excessive glassmorphism.
Randomly colored cards.
Glowing borders around everything.
Saturated orange sections without a compositional reason.
Typography
Preferred fonts:

Display: Geist Sans or Plus Jakarta Sans.

Body: Geist Sans or Inter.

Code: Geist Mono or JetBrains Mono.

Use next/font wherever supported.

Hero typography
Desktop size:

font-size: clamp(3.3rem, 7vw, 7rem);
line-height: 0.98;
letter-spacing: -0.055em;
font-weight: 600;
Adjust based on actual rendering.

A compelling hero does not require oversized text for its own sake. The headline should feel balanced within the viewport.

Section headings
font-size: clamp(2.3rem, 4.5vw, 4.75rem);
line-height: 1.08;
letter-spacing: -0.045em;
font-weight: 600;
Body text
Base size: 16–18px.
Line-height: 1.5–1.7.
Comfortable reading width.
Strong distinction between titles, paragraphs, labels, and supporting details.
Code
13–15px on desktop.
Approximately 1.6 line-height.
Maintain indentation and whitespace.
Use proper syntax highlighting.
Avoid tiny code text to fit more content.
Spacing
Use a consistent spacing scale:

4, 8, 12, 16, 20, 24, 32, 40, 48, 64, 80, 96, 120, 160.

Avoid arbitrary margins and padding across unrelated components.

Containers
:root {
  --page-max: 1440px;
  --content-max: 1200px;
  --reading-max: 720px;
  --gutter: clamp(20px, 4vw, 64px);
  --section-space: clamp(80px, 10vw, 160px);
}
Use a consistent container system.

Desktop:

12-column layout grid.
Generous whitespace.
Controlled text widths.
Strong alignment between sections.
Tablet:

Six-column or appropriately simplified grid.
Mobile:

Single-column composition where required.
Meaningful changes to structure rather than simply shrinking desktop elements.
Borders and radii
Use:

Subtle 1px borders.
10–16px standard card radii.
20–24px hero product preview radius.
Carefully selected button radii.
Soft, minimal shadows.
Avoid using the same giant border radius for every component.

4. PAGE ARCHITECTURE
Trace should have two related but distinct experiences.

Marketing website
Purpose:

Explain the problem.
Establish differentiation.
Demonstrate the product.
Encourage visitors to try a lesson.
Characteristics:

Editorial.
Spacious.
Visually immersive.
Strong typography.
Sophisticated movement.
Carefully art-directed.
Learning application
Purpose:

Help users complete genuine exercises.
Preserve readable code.
Capture predictions.
Submit changes.
Verify results.
Review progress.
Characteristics:

Focused.
Precise.
Accessible.
Efficient.
Lower visual density where possible, but enough density for real technical work.
Both experiences must share typography, colors, UI primitives, and product identity.

Do not force the marketing website's atmospheric gradients into the exercise workspace.

5. MARKETING HOMEPAGE

Recommended eyebrow:

Recommended headline:

You can build with AI.
Now understand the code.

Highlight "understand" in orange.

Primary CTA: Try a lesson

Secondary CTA: See how Trace works

Design requirements:

Large centered headline.
Carefully controlled line breaks.
Compact eyebrow.
Elegant button grouping.
Soft atmospheric treatment.
Generous vertical whitespace.
High-contrast text.
Immediate clarity about the product.
Do not add unnecessary decorative illustrations around the headline.

Section 03 — Hero product showcase
This is one of the most important parts of the entire website.

Show an elegant, realistic Trace application interface below the headline.

The preview should include:

Code excerpt

A realistic Python or FastAPI example.

Example:

"What happens when the request omits the title field?"

Response selection

Examples:

HTTP status.

Response body.
Database state.

Light UI framing.

Small technical labels.
Beautiful but functional controls.

PREDICT FIRST

PRACTISE AGAIN

Immediate access to evidence.

Right:

Verification state.

Code editor

Use syntax highlighting for read-only snippets without loading a large editor engine unnecessarily.

Accurate indentation.
Accessible focus behavior.
Useful line numbers.
Relevant code highlighting.
Clear errors.
Proper selection behavior.

Prediction interface
The learner should commit to a prediction before the outcome is shown.

Do not reveal the answer through premature success styling or hints.

Modification interface
Allow focused code changes.

The interface must show:

Verification interface

Observed behavior

What actually happened.

7. ANIMATION AND MOTION SYSTEM
Motion should elevate the product rather than interrupt it.

Recommended stack
Use:

CSS transitions for simple interactions.
Motion for React for component transitions.
GSAP with ScrollTrigger only when advanced choreography genuinely requires it.
Do not use multiple animation libraries for the same task.

Motion principles
Motion must have a purpose.
Every animated element must remain understandable.
Use consistent easing.
Avoid excessive delays.
Prioritize opacity and transform animations.
Respect reduced-motion preferences.
Never make learning depend on an animation.
Avoid scroll hijacking.
Timing guidance
Microinteraction: 120–220ms.

UI state transition: 180–350ms.

Section entrance: 400–700ms.

Large visual reveal: 500–900ms when justified.

These are starting guidelines, not fixed values.

Hero animation
Suggested sequence:

Navbar gently appears.
Eyebrow fades in.
Headline appears with subtle upward movement.
Supporting copy appears.
CTAs enter.
Product preview reveals.
Keep the entire sequence responsive and avoid making users wait to interact.

Do not hide essential content if JavaScript fails.

Scroll animation
Use small, intentional reveal transitions.

Suitable effects:

Opacity changes.
Slight vertical translation.
Delicate line drawing.
Staggered card reveals.
Progressive trace-path highlighting.
Avoid:

Constant parallax.
Excessive blur animations.
Scroll-controlled text that becomes unreadable.
Decorative animations competing with the code editor.
Long unskippable intro sequences.
Button interactions
Buttons should have:

Immediate hover feedback.
Clear focus indicators.
Short transitions.
Proper disabled states.
Loading indicators when needed.
Lesson transitions
Make state changes purposeful.

Examples:

Predicted outcome becomes committed.
Code change becomes highlighted.
Verification completes.
Expected and observed outcomes become visible.
The next learning step becomes available.
Use subtle motion to reinforce cause and effect.

Reduced motion
Implement prefers-reduced-motion.

Do not depend on a moving effect to communicate information.

Server and Client Components
Use Server Components by default.

Use Client Components when:

A code editor is present.

Follow-up practice.
Persistence.
The frontend should consume the real backend contracts.

Before modifying the integration:

Inspect actual endpoints.
Inspect request and response models.
Inspect authentication or session behavior.
Inspect persistence expectations.
Check available tests.
Preserve known-working behavior.
Do not invent API routes based solely on this document.

Do not replace backend logic with mock frontend state.

Loading and error states
Every asynchronous interaction needs:

Error state.
Recovery action.
Appropriate success feedback.
Stable layout during changes.
Do not show successful verification before the backend reports success.

Security
Do not put secrets in client components.

Validate and safely render untrusted content.

Avoid injecting unsanitized HTML.

Do not execute learner-submitted code in an unsafe application process or browser context.

Keep code execution and verification in the existing approved backend system.

Review at:

360px.
390px.

1920px.

The goal is a visually sophisticated website that remains fast.

Targets for field Core Web Vitals at the 75th percentile:

LCP at or below 2.5 seconds.

CLS at or below 0.1.
Implementation rules:

Use next/font.
Use next/image for suitable image assets.
Lazy-load expensive below-the-fold elements.
Avoid unnecessary JavaScript.
Keep animation transforms GPU-friendly where appropriate.

Dynamically load large editor or graphics libraries.
Avoid excessive blurred layers.
Prevent layout shifts.
Optimize SVGs.
Avoid large background videos unless justified.
The hero should be readable before JavaScript hydration.

Write clearly and specifically.

AI-assisted builders.
Early-stage developers.
People building real applications.
Learners seeking practical independence.

Step 7 — Validate

Step 8 — Report accurately

Trace must succeed on two dimensions.

Design quality
The website should have:

Premium composition.
Strong visual storytelling.
Carefully considered color.
Refined motion.
Cohesive components.
Original product visuals.
Excellent responsive behavior.
Product quality

Teach through realistic code.
Require genuine learner attempts.
Capture predictions.
Support code modifications.
Show verification evidence.
Preserve progress.
Support follow-up practice.
Avoid false learning claims.
A beautiful homepage with a broken learning experience is not successful.

A working learning tool with a generic, poorly composed interface does not meet our design ambition.

Final decision rule
Whenever a design or engineering decision is ambiguous, ask:

Does this help the learner understand code, or does it merely make the interface look impressive?

Choose clarity and correctness first.

Then elevate that experience through outstanding visual design and deliberate interactions.

I saved the document to CLAUDE.md exactly as given and read it back in full (1,478 lines). No CLAUDE.md existed at the repo root, so nothing was overwritten. I haven't changed any code.

The main audit finding is that this repo is an untouched Create Next App starter, and there is no Trace FastAPI backend anywhere on this machine. So there are no lessons, attempts, verification or SQLite yet, and no tests.

1. Trace in my own words
The problem. People who build with AI end up with a FastAPI endpoint that works until it doesn't. Then they can't explain why a request returned 422 or why no database row was written. Prompting for more code doesn't fix that gap in understanding.

The loop. In short sessions of 5–10 minutes, the learner:

reads a small piece of realistic code;
predicts what it will do and commits to that answer before seeing anything;
modifies one expression or a few lines;
verifies against what actually happened: HTTP status, response body, test output, database state;
revisits the concept later in a changed scenario with less help, to see whether it stuck.
Evidence over invented scores. Trace records facts such as "Correct on first attempt", "Hint used before revision", "API returned HTTP 422" or "Revisit completed without hints". It never shows mastery percentages or similar invented scores. Code evidence (what the program did) stays separate from learning evidence (what the learner predicted, tried and later reproduced). Any result not produced by a real run is labelled as a demonstration.

2. Rules I'll follow most strictly
Never fake results. Any evidence that didn't come from the real backend is visibly labelled as a demonstration or sample.
No made-up content. No invented API routes, metrics, testimonials, logos, statistics or mastery scores.
Prediction comes first. The learner commits before any outcome is shown, and nothing hints at the answer early.
Every link and button works. Each CTA goes to a real route or a real in-page anchor; anything unsupported (such as "Sign in") is left out.
Server Components by default. Client code only where there's real interaction, so the hero is readable before JavaScript loads.
Tokens, not one-off colours. All colours come from §3's CSS variables. Orange is a sparing accent and never means success.
Accessibility and motion. WCAG 2.2 AA, keyboard support, visible focus, reduced-motion versions of everything, and no scroll hijacking.
Responsive is part of the design. Checked at 320–1920px, and the page never scrolls sideways except inside code panels.
Minimal dependencies. Nothing gets added without a specific job, and I'll check the Next 16.4 docs bundled in node_modules before using its APIs.
Honest reporting. I only report checks I actually ran in this environment.
3. Audit findings
Area	What's there
Framework	Next 16.4.3, React 19.3.0, TypeScript strict, App Router, src/ layout, @/* alias
Config	cacheComponents, reactCompiler, partialPrefetching and experimental.agentFeedback are on. Tailwind 4.3.3 loads through @tailwindcss/turbopack, with no PostCSS config.
Routes	Only /, which is the starter page.
Components	None: no components/ or lib/ folders.
Styles	globals.css is the starter version: white/near-black colours, a dark-mode switch, and font-family: Arial on body, which overrides Geist.
Fonts	Geist and Geist Mono via next/font/google in layout.tsx. This matches the doc.
Metadata	Still "Create Next App".
Dependencies	Only next, react and react-dom. Motion, Lucide, Radix/shadcn, CodeMirror, Shiki, Playwright and Vitest are all missing.
Backend	None. No Python files, no SQLite, no API client, no env config. I searched the Desktop and your user folder. The FastAPI projects nearby (apex, PayEase) are unrelated (orders, pricing, payments).
Tests	None, frontend or backend.
Assets	Only the starter SVGs (next.svg, vercel.svg, etc.) and the default favicon.
Lint script	eslint with Next's core-web-vitals and TypeScript configs.

No backend. §1, §5.3, §6 and §8 assume a working FastAPI service with verification and saved attempts. For now, every code result on the homepage has to be labelled sample data, and "Try a lesson" has no real lesson to open.

4. Proposed plan for the first homepage (§17 order)

Where is the Trace backend? If it lives in another repo or on another machine, point me to it, because the showcase and CTAs could then connect to real endpoints. If it doesn't exist yet, I'll build everything as clearly labelled sample data.
Where should "Try a lesson" / "Try Trace" go for now? I recommend linking to the interactive demo on the page (#demo) until a real lesson route exists. The alternative is a /learn page, but with no backend it would have to be an honest "coming soon".
What should happen to Desktop\CLAUDE.md? It conflicts with this document and loads into every session here. I'd suggest renaming or moving it, but I won't touch it without your go-ahead.
Can you share the Pinterest reference image? It would make the ambient frame, navbar and hero match what you have in mind much more closely.
Are motion, lucide-react and @playwright/test OK to install?

<\pasted_content id="1721"> You are acting as a senior creative developer, product designer, art director, and frontend engineer working on Trace, a modern coding-learning product.

Your task is to design and implement an exceptional, production-quality frontend experience using Next.js.

FIRST: Read the entire CLAUDE.md file in the project root. Treat it as the authoritative reference for product understanding, design systems, typography, interactions, architecture, and quality standards.

I have also attached a Pinterest website screenshot. This is our PRIMARY VISUAL REFERENCE for the marketing homepage.

THE CREATIVE VISION
I want Trace to look like a premium, carefully art-directed SaaS product, with the refinement of modern Framer and Awwwards-quality websites.

The reference has several qualities I specifically want:

A beautiful, soft, warm gradient environment around the outside of the website.
A large, almost-white internal website canvas with carefully controlled rounded corners.
Floating, elegant navigation with small typography and a clear orange CTA.
A centered, oversized headline with dark typography and selective orange emphasis.
Exceptional whitespace, alignment, section proportions, and visual hierarchy.
A sophisticated product preview beneath the hero, surrounded by subtle ambient lighting and vertical light effects.
Premium feature cards with original visuals and carefully composed content.
Smooth, restrained motion that makes the interface feel alive without slowing it down.
Do not blindly copy the screenshot. Reinterpret its composition, design language, and artistic quality for Trace.

Trace must have its own identity.

UNDERSTAND THE PRODUCT
Trace helps people who build software using AI actually understand the code that AI generates.

Our audience can use AI to create apps, APIs, and backend systems, but they may struggle to predict, debug, modify, and verify the generated code independently.

Trace's learning loop is:

Read real code → Predict behavior → Make a change → Verify the actual result → Revisit the concept later.

This learning experience must inform the visual design itself.

Our interface must not look like a generic online course, quiz platform, or dark terminal dashboard.

It should feel approachable to AI-assisted builders, but technically credible to experienced developers.

FIRST TASK: REPOSITORY AUDIT
Before modifying anything:

Inspect the current project structure and package versions.
Identify the existing Next.js application and routes.
Identify the actual FastAPI integration, if present.
Locate existing learning components, lesson flows, API clients, and reusable design elements.
Determine what is currently functional and what is missing.
Preserve existing product behavior and working backend connections.
Do not rebuild the entire project unnecessarily.

Do not replace functional product features with fake static screens.

DESIGN SYSTEM
Build or refine a coherent, reusable visual system.

Colors
Use the updated color palette from CLAUDE.md.

The primary direction is:

Almost-white background.
Deep graphite typography.
Warm orange accents.
Soft peach, coral, and amber atmospheric gradients.
Subtle gray borders.
Gentle neutral surfaces.
Dark editor surfaces only where they genuinely improve code readability.
Avoid excessive gradients, orange backgrounds ever

The session log now has every prompt you typed, from your first message on 29 October to this one, word for word, with my final reply under each. I rebuilt the whole file with the fixed logger. Nothing is committed or pushed.

**What was wrong**
- **Two automatic summaries were logged as your prompts.** When a long chat gets compressed, Claude Code writes a summary ("This session is being continued…"). It isn't something you typed, so it's no longer logged as a prompt.
- **Two "[Request interrupted by user]" lines counted as prompts.** They're gone; the reply that got cut off is now marked `interrupted_by_user: True`.
- **Pasted text broke the page on GitHub.** Your pasted documents have headings and lists, and GitHub rendered them as part of the log, so prompts and replies blurred together. Each prompt now sits in its own text box and shows exactly as you wrote it. The editor's hidden `<pasted_content>` wrapper is removed; the words inside are untouched.
- **Attachments left no trace.** Prompts sent with screenshots or the guide document now say so ("1 image attached"). The images themselves aren't stored.
- **Headings added.** Each entry now has one, like "Prompt 3 · Muhammad Sohaib · 2026-10-29 14:32 UTC" or "Response 3 · Claude".

The company marker lines (`[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY …]`), frontmatter and statistics are kept as the cohort rule requires.

**Checked**
- 30 of 30 typed prompts are in the log word for word.
- No keys, tokens or email addresses appear.
- The hook works when Claude Code calls it, and with bad input or no input.
- It now updates the log of the session it's called from, so two sessions running at the same time can't overwrite each other's file.
- Running `python .claude/hooks/extract-log.py --all` rebuilds every session log in this project from the start.
- A backup of the old logs and script is in `C:\Users\NexGen\AppData\Local\Temp\claude\c--Users-NexGen-Desktop-trace\ac502e61-757d-4998-a421-8b1428fcceb3\scratchpad\log-backup`.

I didn't change it: it reads its own file back by character count, so changing its layout would break it. If he uses Claude Code instead, he gets this fix when he pulls.

I fixed the CI failure: it came from one flaky backend test, not from your changes. Nothing is committed or pushed.

**What failed:** 1 of 4,268 backend tests, `test_provider_wait_is_bounded_and_timed_out_provider_keeps_subject_lease`, with "Feedback is busy". Your push changed no backend files, and the same commit passed in the pull-request run but failed on push. The Windows job shows "cancelled" only because GitHub stops it as soon as the Ubuntu job fails.

**Why:** the test simulates a slow AI provider call, lets it finish, and then expects the learner's slot to be free again. The app behaves correctly: it holds the slot until the provider's thread has fully finished. The bug is in how the test waits. It runs an empty job on the thread pool, but the pool has several threads, so that job can finish on a different thread before the provider's thread frees the slot. On a busy CI machine that race is sometimes lost.

**Fix:** one change in [test_feedback_operations.py](backend/tests/unit/test_feedback_operations.py). The test now waits with the service's own `wait_for_idle()` method, which blocks until the slot is really released. No app code changed, and the test still checks everything it checked before. This is your teammate's backend test, so let him know.

**Proof**
- **Same failure on demand:** a temporary test slowed the provider's cleanup to mimic a slow CI machine. The old wait failed with "busy" every time, and the new wait passed. That temporary test is deleted.
- **Fixed test:** passed 50 times in a row.
- **Full backend suite, same steps as CI:** 4,268 passed with 95.43% coverage. The `scripts/tests` suite passed 43 of 43, and ruff, format and mypy are clean.

**Local only, not a CI problem:** my first local run failed a different test, because 7 old migration files on your PC still had line endings from before the repo's line-ending rules existed. I re-checked them out from Git with no content change, and that test now passes.

**To push:**
```powershell
git add backend/tests/unit/test_feedback_operations.py .claude-logs/
git commit -m "test(feedback): wait for real provider lease release instead of a pool no-op"
git push
```

Optional: adding `fail-fast: false` under `strategy:` in `.github/workflows/quality.yml` would let the Windows job finish and report its own result instead of showing "cancelled". I haven't changed it; say if you want it.

Task: turn the disabled "Create a custom lesson" feature into a real, polished, easy-to-understand experience. My backend teammate says the LLM and lesson-creation APIs are done, so remove every "Coming soon" for custom lessons and connect the real endpoints. Think as a senior developer and product designer about the whole app: a first-time visitor must always know what to do next. Read CLAUDE.md, `frontend-api-integration.md`, any newer handoff docs, `backend/openapi.json` (regenerate types with `npm run api:types`), `src/lib/ai/*`, the lessons hub, `GeneratedLessonView`, the tutor panel, and the current tests before coding. I'm attaching a screenshot of the current composer.

Rules:
- Frontend only. Don't change backend code, env files, or `openapi.json`. Never put any key in the frontend or `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`. Use the existing API client and adapter (`NEXT_PUBLIC_AI_MODE=real`) with its auth, idempotency, no-store, timeout, and error-envelope handling. Don't automatically replay AI requests after a 401.
- Wire ONLY endpoints that exist in `openapi.json`. If something I need (create request, status, plan approval, list, get lesson, cancel, supported languages) is missing, keep an honest, specific unavailable state for that part only, never fake generation or invent a path, and add it to `docs/frontend-api-requests.md` as a question for my teammate. Mocks are for tests only.
- AI drafts and explains. Never claim a custom lesson was "checked against a real server" unless its `verification_mode` says `server_verified`. Use the shared verification-badge wording for `answer_key` and `practice_only`.
- Keep the approved visual system, motion tokens, and accessibility (WCAG 2.2 AA, keyboard, aria-live, reduced motion, 320 to 1920). Original SVG art only. Don't commit or push.

Information architecture (decide and implement, keep it simple, not simplistic):
1. Navigation: `Lessons` (library) and a primary "Create a lesson" action in the navbar and hub header. Remove dead ends: every page ends with a clear next action.
2. `/learn` (library): a short "Start here" strip for new learners (1. Try a Trace lesson, 2. Create your own, 3. Practice again later) that disappears once they've started; "Continue where you left off"; tabs or sections "Trace lessons" and "My lessons" (status, language, level, verification badge); a prominent create card with example prompts.
3. `/learn/new` (composer page, not a card): one large prompt box with rotating examples and chips; language and framework (suggestions plus free text, from a data table, not hard-coded to Python); level; goal; practice style; lesson length; "Fine-tune" collapsed by default. Replace the Python-only badge with the chosen language. A live "What you'll get" rail that states honestly how this lesson will be checked for the selected language. Privacy line: prompts and any code are sent to the AI provider, don't include secrets or personal details. Draft autosave. Real submit with idempotency key.
4. `/learn/requests/[id]` (progress page): a designed generation tracker (queued, outline, generating, needs review, ready, failed, rejected) with an animated trace-line, estimated wait, cancel and retry where the contract allows, polling with backoff that stops when terminal or the tab is hidden, and a plan-review step if the backend returns an outline (edit titles, remove lessons, change level, "Approve and create"). Friendly specific errors: rate limit with retry hint, quota reached, content-policy rejection with a rewrite hint, provider unavailable, not signed in.
5. `/learn/custom/[lessonId]` (viewer): reuse `GeneratedLessonView` and the tutor panel; code windows in any language (lazy language highlighting, plain monospace fallback); challenge types; verification badges; completion only from server state; "Next lesson" and "Back to library" actions; a "Report a problem with this lesson" link if the contract has one, otherwise a mailto from `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPPORT_EMAIL`.
6. First-run clarity everywhere: plain-language headings (what this page is, what to do next), empty states with one primary button, helpful microcopy instead of jargon, a short "How it works" modal or section reachable from the hub and composer, consistent labels (AI-generated vs Trace-authored lessons are visibly different).
7. Keep it uncomplicated: no more than one primary button per screen, advanced options hidden until asked, no duplicate pages for the same job.

Design quality: this should feel premium, not a plain form. Use the existing hero typography and blur-to-sharp heading reveal, a refined composer with a live preview, a polished generation progress experience, and consistent cards. Check every screen at 1440, 1024, 768, 390, and 320.

Tests: Vitest for composer validation, language data table, each status of the tracker, plan editing, error mapping by code, verification wording per mode, no network call in tests except via the injected adapter; Playwright (desktop and Pixel 7) with the repo's test backend and the backend's mock provider transport only (never real Gemini or a key in tests or CI): hub to composer to progress to viewer, keyboard-only path, new-user and returning-user hub states, no page errors, no horizontal overflow. Keep all existing tests passing and don't weaken assertions.

Verify (don't claim what you didn't run): production build, screenshots of every new screen and status, lint, typecheck, Vitest, `npm run api:types` drift check, Playwright. If prerequisites are met (backend started with the launcher, signed in), make ONE real custom-lesson request end to end and report only status, latency, and whether a lesson came back (never print content or keys); free-tier limits are tight, so don't repeat it.

Final report (under 30 lines): endpoints wired vs missing, files changed, what is real vs still unavailable, the exact verification wording used per mode, test and build results, questions for my teammate. Don't commit and don't push.

The new "Create a lesson" page is built and polished, but creating a lesson still can't work: none of the lesson-creation endpoints your teammate mentioned exist yet. So the "Create lesson" button stays locked with a specific reason, and no request is ever sent. Nothing is committed or pushed.

**Your mid-task change:** the composer is now one chat-style message box with no options at all. You just describe the lesson you want. The language and framework are picked up from your words, so the live preview can still say honestly how the lesson would be checked.

**What's built**
- **Navigation:** on the lessons, today and account pages, the navbar's orange button is "Create a lesson"; it's in the mobile menu too. The homepage keeps "Try Trace".
- **`/learn` (library):**
  - "Start here" for new learners (try a Trace lesson, create your own, practise later). It disappears once you start a lesson.
  - "Continue where you left off", then "Trace lessons".
  - "My lessons", with an honest empty state, example prompts and one "Create a lesson" button.
  - A "How it works" dialog explaining Trace-written versus AI-generated lessons and the three kinds of check.
- **`/learn/new`:**
  - a large message box with rotating examples and example chips;
  - a live preview (detected language, an "AI-generated" tag, the expected check) and a "What happens next" panel;
  - a privacy line, and the draft is saved in this browser.
  - The "Coming soon" text is gone. The button now says: "Creating lessons isn't switched on yet. Trace's lesson-creation service hasn't been published yet, so nothing is sent."
- **Verification wording**, shared in one file and used everywhere:
  - `server_verified`: "Checked on a real server"
  - `answer_key`: "Checked against an answer key"
  - `practice_only`: "Practice only"

  Only Python with FastAPI is shown as "Expected: Checked on a real server", with a caveat; other known languages show the answer key.

**Not built:** the progress tracker (`/learn/requests/[id]`) and the custom-lesson viewer (`/learn/custom/[lessonId]`), as you chose. Without endpoints they'd be pages nobody can reach. 11 contract questions for your teammate are in `docs/frontend-api-requests.md`; they cover create, status, plan review, cancel, list, getting a lesson, languages, check modes, error codes, reporting a problem and privacy.

**Files**
- **New:** `src/app/learn/new/page.tsx`, `src/components/learning/composer/lesson-composer.tsx`, `src/components/learning/how-it-works.tsx`, `src/components/learning/verification-badge.tsx`, `src/content/lesson-languages.ts`, `src/lib/learning/composer.ts`, `src/lib/learning/verification.ts`, plus two test files.
- **Changed:**

  - `globals.css`, `contracts.ts` (proposed types only) and `lesson-source.ts`;
  - `CLAUDE.md` and the docs;
  - 3 existing tests. Two checked the old "Coming soon" form, which no longer exists, and one checked the old empty-state sentence.

**Checks run**

- Vitest: 162 of 162.
- `api:types`: no drift.
- Production build: succeeded.
- Playwright, desktop and Pixel 7: 73 passed, 5 skipped (desktop-only tests on the phone run).

Goal: make custom lesson creation really work, using the SAME Gemini setup that already powers the tutor and Trace Help (private launcher config, existing provider adapter). A learner describes what they want to learn, Trace generates a short structured lesson set, and they study it in the app. Work end to end. Read CLAUDE.md, `frontend-api-integration.md` and any newer handoff docs, `backend/openapi.json`, `backend/app/ai/**`, `core/ai_settings.py`, the tutor and support services, the launcher (`scripts/run_gemini*`), `docs/frontend-api-requests.md`, `src/lib/ai/*`, the lessons hub, `GeneratedLessonView`, and the existing tests first.

Hard rules:
- NEVER print, log, or echo the Gemini key or any env value (names only). Never put a key in the frontend or any `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`. Never log prompts, learner text, or model output. Don't edit tracked example env files except to add NEW variable names (no values).
- Don't commit, don't push, don't touch `.claude-logs/*`. Work on a new branch from the latest `origin/main` (`feat/custom-lesson-creation`) and tell me the name.
- My teammate owns the AI backend. Reuse his provider adapter, bounded client, settings, rate limits, and error envelope. Add code in NEW modules and make only additive changes to his files (registering a router, a service, a settings group). If the feature would require changing his interfaces or behavior non-additively, STOP and report exactly what and why.
- Never execute generated code. Generated lessons are private to the requesting user.
- Free-tier quota is tight and shared with the tutor and Trace Help: tests and CI use the backend's mock transport only (never real Gemini or a key). Total live Gemini calls during this task: at most 3 (one smoke test, no retry loops, never retry on a 429).

Phase 0 (read-only, then continue): check `backend/openapi.json` and `origin/main` for existing custom-lesson, generation, or lesson-request endpoints and any related branch or PR. Report in under 15 lines what exists and what is missing. If a complete public contract already exists, skip Phase 1 and do only Phase 2 against it.

Phase 1: backend (only the missing parts)
1. Endpoints (account or signed-in learner, scoped to the owner, error envelope, no-store, rate limited, Idempotency-Key on create):
   - `POST /api/v1/custom-lessons/requests` returns 202 with a request id (topic, language, framework optional, level, goal, lesson count 1 to 3 for v1, practice styles). Reject empty, oversized, secret-looking, or clearly off-topic or unsafe requests with a typed error and a rewrite hint.
   - `GET /api/v1/custom-lessons/requests/{id}` (status: queued, generating, ready, failed, cancelled, rejected, with error code, timestamps, and lesson ids when ready), `POST .../cancel`.
   - `GET /api/v1/custom-lessons` and `GET /api/v1/custom-lessons/{id}` (the lesson WITHOUT answer keys), `DELETE /api/v1/custom-lessons/{id}`.
   - `POST /api/v1/custom-lessons/{id}/challenges/{challengeId}/answers` (idempotent) checks the submitted answer on the SERVER against the stored key and returns correct or incorrect plus the explanation, so keys never reach the browser. Progress per challenge is stored.
   - `GET /api/v1/custom-lessons/languages` returns supported languages and each one's honest verification capability.
2. Generation runs as a background job (DB-backed status; keep it simple and SQLite-safe; survives a request timeout and a restart: stale jobs are marked failed). The HTTP request returns immediately; the per-call Gemini timeout is its own setting (new variable name, sensible default, well above the 10 s tutor limit), not the tutor's limit. Use the existing provider adapter, with structured JSON output validated by Pydantic. One bounded retry on malformed output, none on rate limits or quota, with spend and token caps from the existing settings and a per-user daily limit on requests.
3. Prompt design: system rules say the user's request is data, never instructions; Python/FastAPI lessons may use the existing runtime concepts but nothing is executed; output = title, objective, short explanation sections, 3 to 5 challenges per lesson (predict the output, find the bug, multiple choice, explain), hints in 3 levels, an explanation per answer, and a `verification_mode` of `answer_key` or `practice_only` per challenge. Never `server_verified`.
4. Validation before saving: schema; the correct option exists and is unique; hints and explanations don't contain the answer text; code length caps; the language tag matches the request; Python snippets parse with `ast` (other languages: length and safety only); no URLs or secret-like strings; reject and mark failed otherwise. Store only what the app needs; no raw prompts or raw model output.
5. Alembic migration for requests, lessons, challenges, and progress (reversible; check `alembic heads` is a single head after rebasing on `main`). Regenerate `backend/openapi.json`; update `docs/frontend-api-integration.md` (new endpoints, auth, errors, statuses, quotas).
6. Tests with the existing fake or mock transport: success, malformed output, validation rejections (answer leak, missing option, bad Python), rate limit and quota errors, cancel, restart recovery, ownership isolation (another user gets 404), idempotency, answer-key endpoint never leaks the key in any response, no prompt or output in logs. Keep coverage above the repo threshold. Run ruff, ruff format, mypy, pytest, Alembic upgrade and check, and the OpenAPI drift check.

Phase 2: frontend
- Run `npm run api:types`; wire the real client methods in `src/lib/ai/` (no duplicate clients; credentials, headers, idempotency, timeout, and error mapping as in the existing client; never replay AI requests after a 401). Remove every "Coming soon" for custom lessons. Keep mocks for tests only, and a visible unavailable state if the backend reports the feature disabled.
- UX (a first-time visitor must know the next step on every screen, one primary button per screen): `/learn` shows "Trace lessons" and "My lessons" plus a create card; `/learn/new` is a full composer page (prompt with rotating examples, language with suggestions plus free text, level, lesson count 1 to 3, practice style, "Fine-tune" collapsed, an honest "how this will be checked" note per language, privacy line, draft autosave); `/learn/requests/[id]` is a designed progress tracker (animated trace line, polling with backoff, stops when terminal or the tab is hidden, cancel and retry, friendly errors for rate limit, quota, policy rejection, provider unavailable); `/learn/custom/[lessonId]` reuses `GeneratedLessonView` and the tutor panel with answers submitted to the server endpoint (no key in the browser), per-challenge badges (`answer_key` or `practice_only`, never "verified"), progress, a "Next lesson" action, and delete. Reuse the existing design system, heading reveal, motion tokens, accessibility (WCAG 2.2 AA, keyboard, aria-live, reduced motion), responsive 320 to 1920.
- Update CLAUDE.md and `docs/frontend-api-requests.md` (mark implemented items, keep open questions).
- Tests: Vitest (composer, each status, error mapping, verification wording, answers via the server endpoint only) and Playwright (desktop and Pixel 7) with the repo's test backend and its mock provider transport: hub to composer to tracker to lesson to answer, keyboard-only path, new and returning user states, no page errors or horizontal overflow. Keep existing tests passing, don't weaken assertions.

Verify (don't claim what you didn't run): backend commands above, then lint, typecheck, Vitest, `npm run api:types` drift check, Playwright, production build, screenshots at 1440x900, 1024, 768, 390, and 320 of each new screen and status. Then ONE live smoke test through the real stack (backend started with the launcher, signed in): create one single-lesson request and report only status, latency, and whether a valid lesson came back (never print content or keys). If it fails, report the error code and stop.

Final report (under 40 lines): what existed vs what you built, endpoints, new variable NAMES (and which file they go in), migration id and head check, limits and caps, test and build results, the live smoke result, anything unverified, and questions for my teammate (including who reviews generated lessons and how long they are kept). Give me the exact `git add`, `git commit`, and `git push` commands to run myself.

The lock isn't a bug in the page. Your backend on :8000 is still the process started yesterday at 20:32, before the custom-lesson code existed. So `GET /api/v1/custom-lessons/languages` returns 404, and the composer correctly shows "not available".

There's a second problem: your database `backend/trace.db` is at migration `0009`, and the launcher doesn't run migrations, so the custom-lesson tables don't exist yet.

I tried to back up the database, stop the backend, migrate and restart it myself, but the permission system blocked stopping your running process. Please run these in the terminal where the backend is running:

```powershell
# 1. Stop the backend (Ctrl+C in its terminal), then:
cd C:\Users\NexGen\Desktop\trace\backend
Copy-Item trace.db trace.db.before-0010.bak     # backup
uv run alembic upgrade head                      # adds the 4 custom-lesson tables only
uv run python -B -m scripts.run_gemini --confirm-free-tier-project --port 8000
```

Then reload `http://localhost:3000/learn/new`.

- **What should change:** the lock should turn into the send arrow once you're signed in. Your request then goes to the tracker page, and the lesson opens when it's ready, usually in about 5–10 seconds.
- **What the lesson is based on:** what you type, plus the language, level, number of lessons and practice styles under "Fine-tune" if you set them.
- **Settings:** you don't need any new environment values, because the defaults apply.
- **If anything goes wrong:** the backup restores your previous database.

If you'd rather I do the restart, allow it and I'll run these steps, then confirm the endpoint and send one test request.

Your teammate's backend code is fine. The lock appears because the backend running on :8000 was started with plain `uvicorn app.main:create_app --factory --port 8000` (PID 31756, started 14:07), not the Gemini launcher. Without the launcher, AI mode resolves to `disabled` with no generation model set. Two things follow from that:

- **The lock:** the composer asks `/custom-lessons/languages` whether generation is available, the backend answers no, and the send button shows the lock. The page is telling the truth.
- **The guide error:** your 4 guide requests were stored as `unavailable` within about 12 ms each. The backend refused locally, before any Gemini call.

The launcher's offline preflight on your machine passes (`ready_for_real_ai: true`, `gemini_free`, credential file valid, frontend in `real` mode). So this is a restart, not a code fix, and no backend issue report is needed.

```powershell
cd C:\Users\NexGen\Desktop\trace\backend
uv run python -B -m scripts.run_gemini --confirm-free-tier-project --port 8000
```

Right now a custom challenge is a one-step quiz (see attached screenshot: "Challenge 1 · Predict the output", options A to D, "Check answer", "Show hint 1 of 3"). Keep the visual design of that custom lesson page, which I like, and use it as the shared lesson shell.

HARD RULES:
- FRONTEND ONLY. Do NOT edit `backend/`, migrations, `backend/openapi.json`, env files, or the launcher. If the loop needs data or endpoints the backend doesn't have, don't fake them: build the UI as far as the real data allows, show an honest specific state for the missing part, and report it (see "Backend gaps").
- Before editing: `git status`, create a backup branch (`git branch backup-before-custom-loop`), work on `feat/custom-lesson-loop`. Don't commit, don't push, leave `.claude-logs/*` alone. Never print secrets, env values, prompts, learner text, or model output beyond field names and structure.
- Never claim "verified by the server" or "ran your code" for custom lessons. Use the existing honest wording: "Answer-key check" (or "Practice only"). The lesson result comes from the lesson's stored data, not from running code. Answer keys must never reach the browser or the tutor context before the learner submits.
- Keep the approved design system, heading reveal, motion tokens, WCAG 2.2 AA, keyboard, aria-live, reduced motion, responsive 320 to 1920, soft-wrapped code (no horizontal scroll).

STEP 1: Study how the Trace lessons really behave (read-only)
Read the real lesson workspace components, hooks, and API usage (`src/components/learning/*`, the lesson pages) and describe in under 20 lines: the states (awaiting prediction, ready for attempt, needs revision, completed), the locked prediction, the in-code edit control and its allowed values, attempts and history, hint slots from summary counts, the evidence panel (expected vs observed vs explanation), completion panel, idempotency keys, and what comes from the server vs the client.

STEP 2: Map the custom-lesson contract
Read `backend/openapi.json`, `src/lib/api/schema.d.ts`, the custom-lesson client, and the newest handoff docs. List every custom-lesson endpoint and the exact shape of a lesson and its challenges: challenge types, options, hints, explanations, any fields for edit options, expected results per option, predictions, attempts, progress, completion, and what the answer endpoint returns. Inspect the STRUCTURE of one of my real saved lessons through the API (field names and types only, never content). Then produce a table: loop step vs available in the contract (yes, partial, no).

STEP 3: Build the loop per challenge (progressive, one clear action per step)
For every challenge, show a stepper (Read, Predict, Change, Verify) and unlock steps in order:
1. Read: the explanation and code (soft wrap, line numbers, any language, "AI-generated, not run" label stays).
2. Predict: learner chooses what they think happens BEFORE anything else unlocks, with an optional "why" box. "Lock prediction" saves it (server if an endpoint exists; otherwise keep it for the session only and say so in one small note). Once locked it can't be edited, and the result is not shown yet.
3. Change: the learner edits INSIDE the code with the same in-code control as the Trace lessons (editable token with constrained options, live line rewrite, diff highlight, Reset). Options come ONLY from the lesson data. If the lesson provides no edit options for that challenge, don't invent any: for "predict the output" challenges with no edit data, use the honest alternative of "Try the alternative": compare your prediction with the alternatives the lesson offers, and clearly say which parts need backend data (see gaps).
4. Verify ("Check my change" or "Check answer"): submit through the real answer endpoint with an Idempotency-Key (reuse the key on uncertain retries, new key for a new deliberate attempt). Show the result in the same evidence layout as the Trace lessons: Your prediction vs What the lesson says happens vs Why (explanation). If the prediction was wrong, say so neutrally and keep it visible next to the result.
5. Revise on a wrong answer (new numbered attempt, history kept, prediction stays locked), hint slots (3, counted from real data), tutor panel available at every step, completion only from server state, then a completion panel (what you predicted, attempts, hints used, concept) with "Next challenge" or "Back to My lessons".
- Challenge types (predict the output, find the bug, multiple choice, explain) each get the closest faithful version of this loop. "Explain" challenges are practice only: show the model explanation after the learner writes theirs, no pass or fail.
- Lesson-level: progress bar across challenges (for example "2 of 4"), resume where you left off after reload or sign-in on another device (from server progress), completion of the lesson only from server state.

STEP 4: Tutor inside custom lessons
Make sure the same lesson tutor panel is present on custom lessons, receives lesson, challenge, and step context (title, explanation, code, the learner's current step and attempts), and NEVER receives answer keys or expected results. Same states (loading, unavailable, rate limit, quota) and privacy line.

Backend gaps (required deliverable)
Create `docs/backend-issue-report.md` listing every missing piece that blocks the full loop, each with: what is missing, evidence (contract line or field), what the UI does meanwhile, and a proposed additive schema. Expected candidates: per-challenge `edit_options` with allowed values and the rendered edited code, per-option `expected_result`, a prediction record (immutable) and attempt history endpoints per challenge, per-challenge progress and completion in the lesson response, hint accounting, answer endpoint response fields (selected, correct, explanation, attempt number). Each challenge in an AI-created lesson now runs as Read → Predict → Change → Verify, like the homepage demo, frontend only, on `feat/custom-lesson-loop`. "Try a change in the code" lets you click a line and type your version inside the code, then press "Check my change ↵ Enter".

One thing to know up front: your typed line isn't checked. The lessons contain no expected fix, so what gets checked is the answer you pick, against the lesson's answer key on the server. Your line is shown as "Your change · Not run" next to the result.

**How the Trace lessons work:** the server holds the state (predict, attempt, needs revision, completed). The prediction is locked on the server, the in-code chip can only produce allowed values, the server runs the check, there are 3 server-counted hints, and completion comes from the server.

**How each custom challenge works now:**
- **01 Read:** the question and the code.
- **02 Predict:** pick an option and say why, then "Lock prediction". It can't be changed afterwards.
- **03 Change:** edit a line in the code (diff highlight, Reset, Enter to check), pick the answer to check, use up to 3 hints.
- **04 Verify:** a real call to the answer endpoint, keeping the same Idempotency-Key on uncertain retries and a new one for each new attempt.
  - The result screen shows your prediction (matched or differed), your change ("Not run"), and the answer-key result, labelled "This result comes from the lesson, not from running your code".
  - The explanation stays hidden until you get it right or click "Show why (gives away the answer)".
- **Revise:** numbered attempts, with the prediction kept.
- **Completion:** per challenge, then the whole lesson, only from server state. After a reload it reopens at the first unfinished challenge.
- **Explain challenges:** practice only. You write, then compare with the lesson's example; there's no pass or fail.
- **Tutor:** a lesson tutor drawer with "Explain this concept", hints and "Why didn't it match?". It never receives the answer key, explanation or example answer.

**What comes from the server vs this browser:**

| Step | Source |
|---|---|
| Read, Verify, completion | server |
| Prediction, attempt details, hints count | kept in this browser per account (no backend fields for them) |
| Change | your line is shown, not run or checked; no edit data in the lessons |
| Tutor | lesson content only; no AI questions in real mode (no endpoint for custom lessons) |

I fixed one bug along the way: finishing a challenge made the page jump straight to the next one, so you never saw your result.

**Backend gaps:** `docs/backend-issue-report.md` lists 6 additive requests with evidence, what the UI does meanwhile, and proposed fields. The message for your teammate is at the end of that file.

**Files:** `src/lib/learning/custom-loop.ts`, `src/components/learning/custom/challenge-loop.tsx` and `custom-tutor.tsx` (new); `custom-lesson-view.tsx` and `globals.css` (changed); tests in `tests/components/custom-lessons.test.tsx` and `tests/custom-lessons-e2e/custom-lessons.spec.ts`.

**Checks:**

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Lint, typecheck | clean |
| Vitest | 231 of 231 |
| `api:types` | no drift |
| Production build | passes |
| Custom-lessons Playwright (wrong then right, tutor, reload, explain, delete; desktop and Pixel 7) | 9 passed, 1 skipped |
| Trace lessons Playwright | 75 passed, 5 skipped |
| Loop screenshots (6 states, 5 widths) | no overflow or page errors |

I shortened the tutor bar's subtitle to "Explain · Hint" after the screenshots; only Vitest covers that change. I didn't run a live check against your real lessons.

**To commit and push yourself:**
```powershell
git add docs/backend-issue-report.md src/ tests/components/custom-lessons.test.tsx tests/custom-lessons-e2e/custom-lessons.spec.ts
git commit -m "feat(custom-lessons): read, predict, change, verify loop with in-code edits and lesson tutor"
git push -u origin feat/custom-lesson-loop
```
